The Belgravian

March 1999

The Belgravian

The official gazette of Belgrave Harriers

Number 227

Front Page Photo: Du'aine Ladejo set his best Decathlon score while competing at the Commonwealth Games in Kuala Lumpur — in spite of an awful long jump that had him on the verge of scoring no points at all in that discipline. Gaining experience in the event all the time, he will surely top 8,000 points in ‘99.

Maybe there's still a hankering to run a few more sparkling 400 metre races though. Du'aine recently won the BUPA B 400 at Birmingham in 46.69, turned up in Ghent on the Ricoh Indoor Tour where he won again in 46.94, and earned selection for the Six Nation International in Genoa.

Back Page Photo: One of the most expressive shots of the year. Belgravian Darren Campbell representing Great Britain & Northern Ireland continues the Linford Christie tradition by winning the European Games 100 metres Championship in a record time of 10.04 seconds.

Pictures by Mark Shearman

Du'aine LadejoDarren CampbellLinford ChristieMark Shearman

Notice · page 2

Stop Press: New Kit Sponsors

As we go to press we learn that Belgrave have new kit sponsors for the next three years. FILA (UK) Ltd. are planning a big push into running in the U.K. and Belgrave falls nicely into their plans with the twin attractions of the finest track & field team in this country as well as the first and still the most progressive Youth Development Scheme.

Submissions have been made for matching SPORTSMATCH funding which, if successful, will lead to an expansion of our Youth Development Scheme as well as the training of more Officials and Coaches to meet the increased demand.

More details from your Team Managers as they emerge.

Notice · page 2

Athens 1999

The 1999 European Champion Clubs Track & Field Competition will be held over the weekend 29th to 30th May in Athens. Belgrave Harriers will be representing the United Kingdom in Division II.

Division I, II and III meetings will all be taking place in the stadium at the same time. Bear in mind that last year athletes of the calibre of Kevin Young, Florian Schwarthoff and Francis Obikwelu were competing for their European Clubs in Division II and you'll realise that a feast of athletics is in store with your club in the thick of it.

Bill Laws is organizing the trip which may well cover Friday 28th through to Bank Holiday Monday 31st. Phone him on [phone number withheld]

Editorial · page 3

Battersea Track gets the Go Ahead

Over the last two years we have reported to you details of our developing relationship and collaboration with Wandsworth Borough Council's Leisure and Amenity Services Department. The major focus of joint plans has been the proposed upgrading of the Battersea Park Track.

It is hard to believe that it was more than two years ago when we originally produced serious plans for upgrading the track to eight lanes with covered spectator facilities and a new pavilion. The inordinately long gestation period has been no fault of Wandsworth's project team but rather has been due to several factors not least of which was a need for the Sports Council to review future development plans for the whole of the Southern Region. During this period lottery funding guidelines became more stringent and in addition there was a good deal of to-ing and fro-ing between the lottery architects and Wandsworth's Planning Department on what constituted a pavilion design worthy of lottery fund support. Put this lot together and you will see that there has been a lot of behind the scenes activity without much to show for it.

You can imagine our project team's frustration especially as time moved closer to the August deadline which, if passed, meant that the project would have to wait yet another year. A delay could have moved us back to 2001 which would have missed all the millennium support for which we were aiming.

But no. Fortunately for everyone the Sports Council finally approved the lottery grant of £2.6 million to Wandsworth Council for the upgrading project in mid August 1998 – just days before the deadline set by the construction programme.

The planned closure of Battersea Track to allow for construction will be from 31st May, 1999 until April, 2000. The official opening is scheduled for June, 2000 and will probably take the form of a British League Division One match followed the next day by an International Inter-City meeting for younger athletes. In this latter event Belgrave officials will be taking a key role in organising the fixture on behalf of Wandsworth Council.

So, it's all systems GO. Exciting times are ahead with Belgrave once again at the centre of the action.

Editorial · page 3

This is madness

We have all come across those very few officials whose main aim during an afternoon's sport seems to be to catch someone out and disqualify them from their competition. Don't they realise that they should be there to assist the athletes, to ensure that they all compete on even terms and comply with the rules that make an athletic achievement directly comparable with another set perhaps at the other end of the country or half way round the globe.

Let's be fair – a disqualification is sometimes necessary. Perhaps someone has false started twice or has gained an unfair advantage through an early dash for the kerb after a baton change; but these are not the occasions to which we refer. No, it's those times when we have already inwardly groaned at seeing a familiar tyrannical figure on the judges stand and sure enough, before the day is out, a stumble by a runner during a middle distance race followed by a pace taken on the grass infield is the trigger for a hardly restrained exclamation of glee and an upraised red flag. You might believe this to be an exaggeration – but we have seen it. We have witnessed a young lad from another local club pulled out of a race for this very offence, with no chance to explain the situation and earn reinstatement because, thanks to this type of judging, the race concluded without him.

As a club we have no special axe to grind. We haven't suffered any more than any other, but there is no doubt that the delight in the disqualification seems to rise in proportion to the prominence of the athlete or the club they represent. It has also been noted that these occurrences seem most prevalent in "age-group" athletics, the very competitions where an athlete's opinions of officialdom are first formed.

Take this incredible but true sequence of events that took place at Peterborough near the end of a women's National Junior League competition last summer:

The team of young women from Luton AC finished third in their 4 x 100 metres race but were then disqualified because, it was claimed, three of their athletes wore vests and the fourth a “crop-top”. The rule quoted was : Rule 17 (2) has been amended to read, “In team or relay races all competitors must wear registered vests of the same design and colour of the team they are representing”.

This is madness. As one of the other officials that day said:

“Surely design is referring to whether there are two horizontal bands or vertical stripes or a diagonal sash. Whether the vests are tucked in, cropped short or indeed built into the shorts in a leotard is irrelevant and not a design matter but one of ‘style’. Are we, now to look for vests with long sleeves and a round neck or scooped neck? All the Luton girls wore yellow with two horizontal black bands. ”

It further appeared that the ruling was not applied to all athletes in the race and not at all to any of the men in other races. The track referee, having made his decision, would not change it though he admitted that he might have acted a little hastily. He said that he had arranged for an announcement to be made reminding athletes of the rule. That announcement said “athletes in the relays must wear the same kit”. Hardly a warning of the stupidity that was to follow.

The loss of 10 points for third place had the dramatic effect of moving Luton AC, at the end of the meeting, down into second place just 8 points behind the host club and with the corresponding loss of a match point. Then there was the ridiculous spectacle of athletes changing vests during the subsequent 4 x 400m relay to ensure that they didn't fall foul of the rule again. So while runner number 2 was on her leg, runner 3 was donning the vest worn by runner 1.

It makes one ashamed to be an official. Thankfully the Executive of the Management Committee of the National Junior Athletic League eventually overruled the track referee that day. Luton AC's relay team were reinstated and they received their 10 points.

Fortunately almost all of the officials with which we come into contact are more enlightened. In Belgrave Harriers our officials are a happy band, slowly growing in number and definitely growing in experience. Some of them have stayed with the sport that they became involved with when their children competed in our teams while others are ex-athletes themselves. All are aware that athletics is for “the athletes” but they take a pride in the fact that without their own help and expertise, true and fair competition among athletes cannot take place. And like many others who take a pride in and enjoy what they do, they are on the lookout for further converts. We could never ever have enough officials. Won't you consider giving it a try yourself. You don't need any experience, we will help you to gain that. Have a chat to the Officials Secretary – Pat Mead on [phone number withheld].

Pat Mead

Feature · page 4

The new governing body of Athletics in the U.K.

John Gebbels of Herne Hill Harriers put together the following brief but very useful summary for the Surrey County A.A. Committee. No doubt it will help many in our own club to understand the current situation.

1. The new body is called U.K. Athletics Limited.

2. There is a Non-Executive Council which consists of:- • The President (Mr. David Hemery) • Two persons elected by the clubs (Sir Eddie Kulukundis and Sir Chris Chataway) • One person nominated by the Sports Council (Mrs Karena Vieck)

It must be explained at this point that the clubs voted for David Hemery as President and that in the interim the others had been appointed by the Steering Committee of Athletics ‘98 which had been set up following the demise of the B.A.F. At the current time the B.A.F. is still in existence although in administration.

3. There will be an Annual Athletic Congress called to discuss whatever business is required. Every two years the President will be elected or re-elected. The President cannot serve more than four years. Every year there will be elections for one of the other Non-Executive Council Member's positions. They also cannot serve for more than four years.

4. An Annual General Meeting will held to:- • Receive a report from the Executive Board • Receive a Statement of Accounts • Appoint Board Members • Appoint Auditors • Transact any other business.

5. The Executive Board will run the organisation on a day to day basis and is headed by the Chief Executive (David Moorcroft). The Board can have from two to ten members and will oversee competition rules, the selection of international teams, control of doping, championships, the commercial interests of the sport including broadcasting, and fixtures.

6. The appointment of sub-committees and determining remuneration of any paid positions will be up to the Executive Board with the agreement of the Non-Executive Board. A full time paid Company Secretary will be appointed.

John GebbelsDavid HemeryEddie KulukundisChris ChatawayKarena VieckDavid Moorcroft

Feature · page 4

Wandsworth Museum Exhibition

Rex Bale

— a look back at sporting activities in Wandsworth.

An exhibition at the Wandsworth Museum (The Courthouse, 11 Garratt Lane) took place during August and September 1998 and had athletics as a prominent feature thanks partly to Editor Alan Mead who provided numerous photographs from our archives, ancient and modern, ranging from the 1928 880yd at the Belgrave Club Championships and the 1934 cross country team, right up to shots taken at recent British League meets. A Belgrave vest was also displayed alongside those of other local clubs.

The borough saw a boom in running in the late 1800s at least as a spectator sport of ‘foot races’. A crowd of 30,000 saw the American Indian Deerfoot take on Alfred Day, a Yorkshireman, over six miles; this took place at the Copenhagen Grounds, Garratt Lane (now built over,) in 1862. Betting on these head-to-head races was the order of the day and match fixing was rife. However, matters came to a head in 1887 (the year that Belgrave Harriers were formed), when a race had to be called off since organisers couldn't agree a winner before the race started! The crowd burnt down the stadium, signalling the end of betting and perhaps the beginning of wider participation in running as a sport? Certainly a poster on display in the exhibition showed that two years later for the price of six (old) pence (just over 2p) you could see a 20 - race event at Balham Athletic Ground “5 mins from Clapham Common”.

There have certainly been some classy runners in our area in the past; for example Ernest Pelling of Tooting (who first introduced the crouch start), held the world record at 200 yards, Albert Hill also of Tooting, was the 800m champion in the 1920 Olympics, at Antwerp, and Edgar Mountain of Streatham was fourth in the same race.

The Wandsworth borough can be proud of its record in the development of other sports too. In cycling the Putney velodrome was the first cement cycling track in Britain and had a crowd capacity of 8,000. In swimming, the Tooting Bec Lido is the largest in London, and in cricket Putney Cricket Club produced England stars, Jack Hobbs and Jim Laker.

Rex BaleAlan MeadDeerfootAlfred DayErnest PellingAlbert HillEdgar MountainJack HobbsJim Laker

Notice · page 5

Important notice regarding Subscriptions

As was agreed at the Annual General Meeting, 1998, the date that subscriptions for membership become due will be moved from 1st July to 1st April.

The reason for this change is that the vast majority of our Club expenditure, including the payment for the use of tracks and the hire of coaches for travel, becomes due in the first quarter of the year. With subscriptions previously not due until the middle of the year and their actual receipt by the Club being spread over several weeks (and dare we say in some cases, months) after that, we have annually experienced a severe cash flow problem. Moving the due date to 1st April should go a long way towards solving the problem.

This means that when you renew your subscription in 1999 it will be the last time that you do so on 1st July because in 2000 you will need to pay on 1st April.

We appreciate that subscriptions for the period commencing 1st July 1999 will only cover 9 months but it is hoped that everyone will cooperate in paying the full amount.

Column · page 5

From The Belgravian

25 Years Ago ... 1974

THE COMMONWEALTH GAMES, CHRISTCHURCH, NEW ZEALAND. Halfway round the World, Belgrave were well represented in the tenth British Commonwealth Games.

What a line up the 3000 metres Steeplechase produced – Bejamin Jipcho the World Record holder, Amos Biwott the Mexico Olympic Champion, Evans Mogaka who was ranked 6th in the World in 1973, and amongst those from these Isles our own John Bicourt who so narrowly missed the British Record last year. John suffered a throat infection at Christmas and it still hadn't cleared up as the Games approached. The team doctor diagnosed the trouble as a touch of bronchitis and with only nine days to the race advised no running for three to five days!

The Kenyans made the running with the Welshmen Davies and Hayward following. John, not feeling too confident after the doctor's advice, played it safe and kept back down the field. At the halfway mark Biwott was dropping back but our man was still running steadily in 7th place. Running the race of his life, Davies stepped up the pressure with two laps to go. By now John was up with Hayward about 40 metres behind the leaders.

Shortly after the ‘bell’ the incident occurred in which Mogaka and Davies fell, leaving Jipcho to run home comfortably. They were both soon up again and heading hard for the finish but John Bicourt too was now moving really fast. He had overtaken Hayward with just over a lap left and by the time the finish was reached there was a margin of six seconds between them. Mogaka, just out of reach, crossed the line in bronze medal position ahead of him. 1, B.Jipcho (Kenya) 8:20.8 (Games Record); 2, J.Davies (Wales) 8:24.8 (U.K. Record); 3, E.Mogaka (Kenya) 8:28.6; 4, J.Bicourt (England) 8:29.6.

The 20 miles Walk was held over an out-and-back course with a 5 p.m. start to avoid the worst of the sun and out of the fifteen starters, four were Belgravians with Len Duquemin and John Moullin for Guernsey, Carl Lawton for England and Allan Callow representing the Isle of Man.

England's Warhurst and Thorpe led all the way. Allan was in the second group for a while but gradually fell off the back. John Moullin was going steadily until the turn when he found the going extremely tough due to the head wind and the cold. Len Duquemin also suffered but it was Carl Lawton who again faded on the big occasion. He was 7th at the turn in 79:25 but slowed drastically before eventually retiring at 14 miles. 8, A.Callow 2:53:12.2; 9, L.Duquemin 2:53:37.4; 10, J.Moullin 2:57:27.2.

Dramatic events also took place in the Christchurch stadium when Barbara Lawton, at that time not a Belgravian, became Commonwealth Champion with a games record in the high jump. While athletes outside the stadium waited to march in for the closing ceremony, four athletes were still involved in the competition at 1.80m. At 1.82m, they were down to three – two Canadians and Barbara who needed two attempts to stay in. Up to 1.84 and here it was resolved as Barbara flew over the bar at her first try.

and 50 Years Ago ... 1949

ENGLISH CROSS COUNTRY CHAMPIONSHIPS. March 12th 1949. ... we journeyed to Birmingham for the “National,” held on the Bromford Bridge Racecourse ...

On the first small lap of the senior race Vern Blowfield was up to the front with Aaron, the eventual winner, but could not hold his position and gradually fell back. Our other men were well placed to begin with but as each lap came round, with the exception of Steve Charlton, they seemed to go further and further back while our most dangerous rivals, Sutton Harriers, had their men well up and going forward all the time.

We soon realised that we had lost our title of “National” champions, but must say that the Sutton lads deserved their win and are indeed worthy champions.

Vern Blowfield was our first man home in 18th, and Steve Charlton did well in being 58th.

In the Junior race Eddie Short again ran well ... he was our first man home in 13th place. C.W.Walker, the winner of our recent road race for Novices, did well in his first “open” race to finish in 34th position, but we missed the services of Arthur Bruce, who could not get leave from his unit.

THE BRIGHTON RELAY. April 2nd 1949. Starting from Mitcham, our runner on the first stage to Purley was Derrick Burfitt. He did well to finish 5th. On the next stage to Merstham, Ted Stimpson held his place ... then Frank Abberton tackled the uphill stage through Redhill and ran a fine race to take us up to 2nd place.

On the next stage Jack Brown took us in front. Len Herbert went off on the 5th stage. Jimmy Wood, our Poly. member, caught and passed him but Len managed to hang on to 2nd place and handed over to Albert Frost who did well in putting up the fastest time for that leg. Vern Blowfield went off on the long stage to Handcross and soon passed his man to put us in the lead once again. Len Marchant was our next but he lost the lead to Green of Reading. Eddie Short went off on the up-hill climb to Patcham. He passed the leader and gave us a lead of 11 seconds over our nearest rivals. Bill Lucas, running the last stage, as usual, did not give his opponents any chance of getting on his shoulder and although Green of Birchfield took his team into 2nd place, Bill finished an easy winner. 1, Belgrave 3 hrs. 57 mins. 35 secs; 2, Birchfield, 3.57.52; 3, Tipton H., 3.58.35.

This makes our 6th win in this classic road race, a feat which no other club has performed and moreover, is our second series of three wins in a row as we won in 1934-5-6 and then again in 1947-8-9.

WORLD RECORD TRACK WALK. Harry Churcher, as is now well known, succeeded in lowering his world's record time of last year for the 5 miles. Walking in excellent style, Harry lapped the track time after time in almost identical times, eventually clipping 10.4 secs. off his time at Motspur Park.

Harry's time, not reported in the Belgravian, was 35 mins. 33 secs., the race being held at London's White City on June 16th.

Bejamin JipchoAmos BiwottEvans MogakaJohn BicourtJ.DaviesHaywardLen DuqueminJohn MoullinCarl LawtonAllan CallowWarhurstThorpeBarbara LawtonVern BlowfieldAaronSteve CharltonEddie ShortC.W.WalkerArthur BruceDerrick BurfittTed StimpsonFrank AbbertonJack BrownLen HerbertJimmy WoodAlbert FrostLen MarchantGreenBill LucasHarry Churcher

Feature · page 6

Patricia Mead

President 1998~99

There was that noise once more—the rumble of roller skate wheels on cracked paving stones—it was that skinny kid with the dark ringlets and short dress again, threading her way through the back streets that lie between the busy Kennington Park and Walworth Roads, weaving in between the jaunty, foraging dog and old lady out shopping, past the oil shop and the corner newsagent's, heading for the newly laid tarmacadam of Lorrimore Road. It was school holiday time and in the mid-1950s with no parked cars and only the milkman's or coalman's horse and cart to avoid, this was a young skater's idea of heaven. And when the delights of Lorrimore Road had been sampled, seeing that it was Friday, perhaps she'd make her way across to Kennington Park, opposite the pile known as “The Horns”, where the travelling cinema would be set up and scores of kids would be sitting on the grass watching the films of “local boy made good” — Charlie Chaplin.

Patricia was used to looking after herself. Dad, an ex-Desert Rat, had been a boxer in the army. When she came home from school on one occasion complaining about the actions of some bullyboy, he taught her how to square up and then, offering his own broad nose as a target, showed her how to throw a straight left that would bring tears to the eyes of any assailant. It proved to be a useful lesson.

Growing up in an inner London borough in the decade after the war seemed no particular hardship at the time. The Elephant & Castle had been all but flattened and there were scores of bomb-sites to play in. Only slowly being redeveloped, these places offered a refuge among the commercial sprawl of South London for caterpillars, birds, colonising buddleia and other floral escapees from urban gardens. As the seeds had taken root in the rubble, so they also took a hold in the interest of a young girl who to this day is never happier than when pottering about in the garden; and the adventurous roamings through the streets led to an independence and spirit that shines through when standing up for her club and managing her team in the Rosenheim League.

All sports seemed to have an attraction for young Patricia, netball and swimming being particular favourites, but after showing promise as an 11-year old in the South London Schools athletics championships, her sports master at Stockwell Manor bought her a pair spikes from the school funds and directed her to the local athletic club—Selsonia Ladies AC.

At Selsonia, one of the top London women's clubs of the '50s and '60s, she soon started competing in track & field matches at high jump and sprints and, eventually, even road relays. There were some famous names in their teams and today, some forty years later, she is pleased that she can still number international sprinter Marianne Dew (now Black) and four times National Cross Country Champion Pam Davies among her friends.

One other sport took hold of Pat in her teenage years—soccer.

Taken to White Hart Lane by an uncle one December Saturday in 1957, she was one of the 27,855 crowd who saw Tottenham Hotspur thrashed 1-4 by Leicester City. The home team had lost but they had gained another fanatical supporter. After Christmas she visited White Hart Lane again to savour the revenge of Leicester being put out of the Cup by her team and before long every winter Saturday was spent on the terraces at home or away.

In the years that followed, as Spurs became the dominating English team, Pat supported them at reserve matches, saw them become the first team ever to win the F.A. Cup and League double, queued up at the turnstiles overnight for cup tickets and in a match to remember in September 1961 saw Danny Blanchflower lead the Spurs to an 8-1 drubbing of Polish Champions Górnik Zabrze. Great days and great players—Bill Brown, Ron Henry, big Maurice Norman, Dave McKay, John White, Terry Dyson, Les Allen ... the names trip off the tongue even today.

By the time she was 16 and training regularly at Battersea, Pat met up with Tom Carter's lads. Tom encouraged her and a friend to come along to Belgrave Hall for a social event and thus began her long association with Belgrave Harriers. Training sessions at Battersea included starting practice with Frank Simmons' group, among them Dave McMillan, while Monday nights were given over to circuit training at Balham with John Martin, Bill Couzens and Brian King. Before long she was cooking hot dogs and brewing the tea after a run on Tuesday and Thursday evenings at the Hall, helping with the production of The Belgravian, following the London-Brighton Road Relay in the Belgrave supporters' bus, helping get the results out at Track & Field and Cross Country meetings, joining the pirate gang to entertain children at the Belgrave Christmas Party and getting involved in Charlie Manning's hare-brained ideas of entertainment at Belgrave social functions.

The Southern Counties A.A.A. decided to look for someone to assist Arthur Kendall in the office at Park Crescent in London shared with the A.A.A. and B.A.A.B. — the base from which almost every athletic activity in the country was governed. Alf Harley and Bill Lucas knew just the person to fill that role and soon Pat was working with Tony Ward and later Andy Norman, helping to organise the Southern Counties Championships and the famous Open Meetings—a position which enabled her to champion the cause of several up and coming young Belgravians when it came to selections for representative matches. Even in recent years, when the last major meetings were held at the Crystal Palace, Pat was still called upon to operate the electronic scoreboard.

But back in the late '70s, things were not going well for Selsonia. Talks were held between the struggling ladies' club and Belgrave Harriers, with a view to a possible merger, but Selsonia decided against it and then when things got worse they finally disbanded. Pat, Barbara Lawton and Jane Pritchard had already joined the ‘men only’ Belgrave Harriers as Associate Members and the three of them ran from the Hall even though the Club had no official Ladies Section. Then, when Belgrave officially opened it's doors to women in 1976 they all signed up as full members in the first week. Changing and washing accommodation was a small area now occupied by the women's shower cubicles—except that there were no showers, only a toilet and hand basin.

Soon some twenty-odd girls had joined Belgrave. Bill Couzens spent his Friday evening's organising weight and circuit training sessions at the Hall while the following summer Ray Hall stepped in to organise our first Women's T&F Team. The original Belgrave women's racing strip—claret with gold edging and a central Belgrave badge—was designed and made by Pat and this style of kit has only recently been updated. The Club had hoped that she would become involved in the management of our fledgling Women's team but by then another project came along requiring a few years maternity leave, although the Club continued to call on her services on numerous occasions when results needed to be produced—particularly the Belgrave/Southern 12-Stage Road Relay.

However, when we reorganised the way we ran our club in 1993 and tried to put a Team Manager in place for every single team, Pat took over responsibility for the Rosenheim League. In the 30 years of this league's existence we had never ever won the men's final and had probably hardly ever won a match. In her first year we won 3 matches and, more importantly, the final. Since then we have not only won the Eastern Division and Final five consecutive times but have won 28 of the last 29 matches. Our longest winning streak is 20, and that only came to an end when, in a match held during a monsoon, she put the athletes' safety first and pulled them out of several of the dangerous events. The men's Rosenheim League success has been based solidly on filling out the team with 17 to 20 year old lads with a sprinkling of veterans; not only has she grown very fond of her boys but they in turn have a high regard for her and in spite of winding her up by pretending that they cannot run another step, still turn on the fireworks for the evening's finale—the 4 x 200 metres relay.

Pat is our Officials Secretary for home Track & Field matches, is a Grade II Track Judge and is almost always our Track Referee for home matches. She has strong views about the way some officials relate to the athletes, always remembering that the officials are there for the benefit and assistance of the athletes, guiding them into competing within the rules rather than looking for the slightest infringement for which a they can be disqualified. Time is still found for the odd veteran competition, although she now prefers the throws, and that fighting spirit is still there, taking her recently to 10th ranked W50 discus thrower in the country.

Patricia Mead — a worthy addition to the President's Board at Belgrave Hall.

Pat on Rosenheim Team Managerial duties at Tooting, 1998.

Early Belles - 1976 at Battersea Park. Left to right are: Wendy Richardson, Jane Pritchard, Anna Matthews, Tina Couzens, Joan Samuels, Pat Mead and Win Shepherd.

Obituary · page 8

S.G.Scarsbrook

ARM

Obituaries

We were sorry to hear, last September, of the death of Stan Scarsbrook, just one month short of his 90th birthday.

Stan joined Belgrave Harriers in 1993 but it was more a case of ‘rejoining’ as he had been a member of Surrey Athletic Club back in the 1930s. Upon joining he told us that as a young man he would,

“ ... run anywhere—it was great. Club matches, relays, cross country and road running, London to Brighton Relay ... ”

What he didn't tell us at that time was just how good he had been in his competitive days when his main event was the steeplechase. It seems that some of his success was due to the fact that he was one of the first men, in this country at any rate, to traverse the steeplechase barriers by lightly stepping on top of them as opposed to vaulting them.

It was in 1934 that Stan first came to prominence when at the age of 25 he was selected to represent Great Britain vs. France in Paris on 29th July. In the 3,000 metres steeplechase he was narrowly beaten by R.Rerolle of the home team, 9:27.4 to 9:29.2. It was one of only two track events won by the French that day although the host nation was very strong on the field and the overall result came out at 66 points to Great Britain and 53 points to France.

Soon afterwards came the A.A.A. Championships at the White City where, in the 2 miles steeplechase, Stan headed the field with 10:48.4, thereby earning himself selection for the Empire Games to be held just three weeks later.

Now at the top of his form, the Surrey A.C. man struck gold again and was one of only four A.A.A. champions to repeat their victories at the ‘Games’, also held on the White City cinders, the others being Rampling (440 yds), Lovelock (1 mile) and Finlay (120 yds hurdles).

The following year saw the first ever home international match staged outside England as Great Britain took on Finland in a meeting held in Glasgow. Here Stan came up against the reigning Olympic Steeplechase Champion Volmari Iso-Hollo who had no trouble in disposing of the Britons in the 2 miles event with 10:15.4. Stan was third man home and first for his country.

Although living in Peeblesshire in Scotland, Stan avidly followed the fortunes of Belgrave Harriers on television. We mourn the breaking of another link with the early days of our sport.

Stan ScarsbrookR.RerolleRamplingLovelockFinlayVolmari Iso-HolloARM
See it on the printed page →
Obituary · page 8

Rose Morrell

Regretably, we must also record the death on 17th July 1998 of Rose, wife of the late Tom Morrell and mother of Mavis Hall. Rose had survived Tom by just three years and eight hours.

Theirs was a partnership upon the like of which our club has been built. Rose supported Tom in all the numerous tasks he undertook for Belgrave Harriers. When Tom was in charge of club kit sales it was Rose who fielded many of the 'phone calls and took the orders—and when one ordered a track suit bearing the name BELGRAVE it was Rose who painstakingly stitched each individual letter onto the shoulders.

Our condolences go to Mavis and son-in-law Eric Hall and their children.

Rose MorrellTom MorrellMavis HallEric Hall

Obituary · page 8

John F. Keown

Gordon Doubleday

We were all sad and sorry to hear of the death of John F. Keown on December 6th 1998.

John was born in 1931 and joined the Club in 1954. He was a regular in our walking teams over all of the recognised courses and distances. He was a good clubman and his performances seemed to improve as the distances increased. Quiet and unassuming he gave his best right up until he had crossed the finishing line. One of his greatest performances was in the 1971 Ewhurst 100 miles when he completed the distance in 20 hrs. 49 mins. 47 secs. to become Centurion number 473. (He was accompanied by fellow Belgravian Johnny Morris, number 474, and they walked and finished together.)

John's knowledge of the sport and its participants was great and at some time or other he filled just about every administrative position in the Southern Race Walking Association and National Race Walking Association, including being elected President of both bodies. He was Race Walking representative on the British Athletics Federation Committee and a Life Member of Belgrave Harriers. Such was his administrative ability that he could turn up for a race and with no more than a single sheet of paper and a pencil, proceed to carry out the lap scoring, record the times and produce a thoroughly accurate result in next to no time.

Readers of the “Race Walking Record” will recall the many articles he wrote such as “Is team competition going out of fashion?” “The R.W.A. is 90 years old” and “1958-69 The British glory years”.

By profession John was a Civil Servant with Customs & Excise, specialising in VAT.

It was always a pleasure to meet him and have a chat (about walking matters, of course!). He will be sadly missed but not forgotten.

John F. KeownJohnny MorrisGordon Doubleday

Obituary · page 8

Percy William 'Jimmy' Green

ARM

If there is one thing that virtually all athletes in Britain have in common, it's that weekly ritual of browsing the pages of Athletics Weekly to find out what the opposition are doing while we are injured, searching for likely races to enter and generally keeping up to date with the sport. On March 23rd 1998, the man who founded this institution, P.W. ‘Jimmy’ Green, passed away at the age of 88.

‘Jimmy’ began his athletic life as a sprinter with Surrey AC in 1924 but soon moved on up through the distances to represent the A.A.A., R.A.F. and several counties at the middle and longer distances, eventually competing in the 1948 Polytechnic Marathon. As a Belgrave Harrier during the 1939-45 War he was a key member of our teams that dominated the 2 mile track races that were held regularly at that time.

After the War, and in spite of advice to steer clear of such a risky enterprise, ‘Jimmy’ went ahead after being demobbed from the R.A.F and started the monthly magazine Athletics in 1945. It was hard work but believing in what he was doing he persevered and by 1950 the magazine expanded to become a weekly publication and took the name by which we have known it ever since.

Editing Athletics Weekly after his competitive days was far from his only involvement with athletics, however. As a coach he was particularly successful, bringing several of his athletes through to international level. He was also a Grade I Starter, a meeting organiser and Past President of Kent County A.A. Athletics was in his blood, further proved by the fact that his son and grandson both became active athletes.

As a life member of Surrey AC ‘Jimmy’ came back into the Belgrave fold when the two clubs merged in 1982 and taking into consideration his time with the former, he was our longest serving member at around 73 years.

Percy William GreenARM

Obituary · page 8

Ron Severn (1926-1998)

Gordon Biscoe

It is with much sadness that we have to announce the sudden death from a heart attack, of Ron Severn, aged 72 years.

Ron was for many years a keen road runner with Wycombe Phoenix Harriers, competing in many of the classic races of the time such as the Finchley 20. He later went on to organise and generally mastermind the promotion of the famous Wycombe 5m Road Race, perhaps more popularly known as ‘The Furniture Five’. This was a race where many teams and stars of international standing would compete for the much sought after prizes of pieces of furniture. Regularly amongst these teams was of course Belgrave Harriers led on many occasions by our own Gerry North.

It was in 1983 that, after having moved to Wimbledon with his family, Ron actually discovered Belgrave, a name he had been all too familiar with in the past. To be correct it's never really been established as to whether it was Ron who did the discovering or his dog! For it was while on a dog walking stroll from their flat on Wimbledon Hill, that one of them led the other into Denmark Rd, resulting in Ron returning home to announce that he had found that Belgrave Harriers were based just round the corner. Ron quickly introduced himself to us and the rest has become history. Belgrave had acquired another top class organiser and official.

During his fifteen years with us Ron put more into the Club than some people achieve in a lifetime. For several years he took on the unenviable task of Treasurer then as an Auditor and, for the two years prior to his death, organiser of our annual Dinner/Dance. On many occasions over the years he regularly officiated at Road, Cross Country and Track fixtures filling any post where help was needed most. By profession he was a qualified accountant and a Chartered Secretary for a large Company in the City, and on more than one occasion his professional expertise was put to the Club's benefit. Perhaps however Ron's ‘master stroke’ came in 1986 when, following up on an article he had read in a Sunday supplement, Sir Eddie Kulukundis was invited to become our Patron. Again, the rest has now become part of the Club's history. For his overall services to Belgrave, Ron was made a life member in 1989.

It can be safely said that he did not ‘suffer fools gladly,’ his maxim being that if a job was worth doing , then it should be done properly. However to know Ron was to realise that this no nonsense professional approach to all that he did belied a warm and almost impish sense of humour. Away from athletics Ron was an avid traditional jazz enthusiast, as well as enjoying a wide range of music from Choral to Frank Sinatra. In his younger days Ron had been a musician himself playing trumpet in a small dance band. In later life he became a professional Toast Master for eight years and, in the weeks only just prior to his death, had been accepted as a Wandsman (Usher) at St. Paul's Cathedral.

Ron was truly a man of many interests, one who is, and will be, missed by many people. Belgrave Harriers extend their sincere condolences to his wife Pauline, daughter Lesley and son Martin.

Ron SevernGerry NorthEddie KulukundisFrank SinatraPaulineLesleyMartinGordon Biscoe

Report · page 9

The Hon. Secretary's Report to 1998 Annual General Meeting

Charles Henn, Hon. Secretary

Sadly, four members of the Club died during the last twelve months: Gwen Cousins, Jimmy Green, Ron Severn and Stan Scarsbrook, as also did Rose Morrell.

Once again, Belgrave have had a very successful year in athletics competition.

I will start with track and field.

Our senior men, under the team management of John Jeffery and Bill Laws, have again won the First Division of the British League by a big margin, winning every match but one; and once again they have gone on to complete the double with the Cup. Thanks to the generosity of our patron, Sir Eddie Kulukundis, who met the travel costs, Belgrave were able to take part in the European Championship Clubs (Second Division) meeting at Belgrade in May, for which they had qualified last year, finishing a creditable fourth against some very strong opposition.

Our strength in depth has been shown by the success of our men's Southern League team, managed by Bob Bridges, who won all their matches in Division 2 and have achieved promotion to Division 1. With many of our juniors moving into the senior ranks next year we see the need again for a second men's Southern League team.

Once again we have, with Pat Mead as team manager, won our division in the Rosenheim League – indeed winning every match – and have gone on to win the League final for the fifth successive year.

Sadly our women's Southern League team, managed by Cynthia James and Jacqui Smiter, who were promoted to Division 2 at the end of last season, have not been able to hold on to their place and are headed back down to Division 3. They were second in their first-round match in the Jubilee Cup competition, but in that competition only the winners progressed. The U15 section of the team has been particularly weak, and we are trying to strengthen it for next season – e.g. by introducing Sportshall athletics during the winter to provide continuity throughout the year: This is something that would, of course, apply to boys as well as girls.

Eight first-claim members of the Club were selected for the European Championships at Budapest. Darren Campbell's win in the 100 metres in 10.04 seconds must have pride of place, with Dwain Chambers taking the silver medal; Darren was also one of the winning quartet in the sprint relay. Matt Yates finished 6th in the 1500 metres and Brendan Reilly 9th in the High Jump. Andy Tulloch reached the semi-finals of the 110m hurdles, Chris Rawlinson took part in the 400m hurdles, Sean Baldock was one of the team in the heats of the 4x400m relay and Nick Sweeney threw the discus for Ireland.

There were four Belgravians in the British team in the World Cup match at Johannesburg: Dwain Chambers was third in the 100m, in 10.03 seconds and ran also in the winning sprint relay team: Sean Baldock was one of the four who finished second in the longer relay; Mick Jones was 5th in the hammer and Mike Edwards 6th in the pole vault.

Most of the athletes I have mentioned also took part in the Commonwealth Games which have just finished at Kuala Lumpur and indeed Darren Campbell and Dwain Chambers won gold medals in the sprint relay and Mick Jones the silver medal in the hammer. They were joined by yet other members of the Club; Ben Challenger, who won the silver medal in the high jump, John Regis who took the bronze medal in the 200m, Du'aine Ladejo in the decathlon, Jason Lobo over 800m, Dave Smith in the hammer and Ian Tullett in the pole vault – all of these in the English team – plus Matt Douglas for Northern Ireland in the 400m hurdles, Lee Newman in the discus for Wales and Dave Pamah in the 1500m for St. Vincent and the Grenadines. By my count 15 Belgravians in all were there.

Six members of the Club competed for Great Britain in the U23 International against Germany in August.

At the U20 level our men under John Powell's team management won the Southern Premier Division of the National Junior League in their first season after being promoted into it, and were indeed an excellent third in the national final. No other club in the history of the League has done so well in the national final in their first year in the Premier division. As for the younger age-groups, there was a bumpy start for our team managed by John Dawson, with assistance from John Powell, in the McDonalds Young Athletes League, but they improved markedly and indeed won their last match; they did not qualify for the national finals but they have retained their place in the Southern Premier Division.

Darren Thompson, who won the English Schools long jump championship, went on to represent Britain at the World Junior Championships at Annecy in France, where Chris Lambert also took part (in the sprint relay). Austin Ferns represented Great Britain in Spain in July over 400m hurdles.

Among the veterans, the men, managed by Terry Lawton, were third or fourth in each of their matches in the Southern Veterans' League, but our veteran women, managed by Maureen Jones, won three of their four matches and reached the South of England final.

I turn to cross-country and road running. The welcome improvement reported last year among the senior men, managed by Alan Mead, has been maintained. They finished 4th in the Surrey League and won both team awards, for 4 and 8 to score, in the South of the Thames Junior race. This year they were only third team in the Surrey Championships, but Belgrave again provided the individual champion – on this occasion, Kassa Tadesse. Kassa also won the South of the Thames Senior race in which, indeed, we had the first three men home and took both the team awards (6 to score and 12 to score) by considerable margins. On the road, Paul Evans ran 2:09:20 for 5th place in last October's Chicago Marathon, and in November, Kassa Tadesse became A.A.A. and Inter-Counties champion over 10km in the Barnsley road race.

The Belgrave women were, sadly, relegated to Division 2 of the Surrey cross-country League, but Gabby Collison ran a number of good races on the road, including 8th place in the A.A.A. 10k and a Club record half-marathon in 80:57.

Though perhaps not as successful as last year, our young walkers under the management of Steve Whorlow remained prominent on the national stage, and Nigel Whorlow was awarded two British junior international vests.

These results reflect great credit, of course, on the athletes concerned, but they also owe a great deal to the team managers, who have borne the burden of getting teams together, persuading people to turn out, arranging transport and being there on the day to encourage and to sort out problems. They deserve all our thanks. So, too, do our coaches who work throughout the year to help Club members to develop, and also those who give up their time to act as judges, starters, timekeepers, recorders at athletics meetings – without such officials there would, indeed, be no athletics meetings.

As I say, we owe our gratitude to all those who give so much of their time and effort to help in these and other ways. I'm not saying this for myself, since I myself do relatively little, but as Secretary I see a lot. All too often it is the same people who double up on these jobs, frequently having to earn a living as well, and some of them also serve the Club on committees. It is worrying that there are so few. If any others of you can see your way to joining in, or know of people who might be willing, do please let us know. The Club can pay the cost of the courses necessary for people to qualify as coaches and officials. More help will be very welcome.

Gwen CousinsJimmy GreenRon SevernStan ScarsbrookRose MorrellJohn JefferyBill LawsEddie KulukundisBob BridgesPat MeadCynthia JamesJacqui SmiterDarren CampbellDwain ChambersMatt YatesBrendan ReillyAndy TullochChris RawlinsonSean BaldockNick SweeneyMick JonesMike EdwardsBen ChallengerJohn RegisDu'aine LadejoJason LoboDave SmithIan TullettMatt DouglasLee NewmanDave PamahJohn PowellJohn DawsonDarren ThompsonChris LambertAustin FernsTerry LawtonMaureen JonesAlan MeadKassa TadessePaul EvansGabby CollisonSteve WhorlowNigel WhorlowCharles Henn

Feature · page 10

Nothing new ...

People

A letter tucked away in our archives dated 30th December 1929 recently came to light. It was written by J. W. Neville, Hon. Secretary of the Athletic Section of St. George's Services Sports Club, and addressed to W.L. Bayliss, Hon. Secretary of Belgrave Harriers. It reads ...

“Dear Sir,

It has come to my notice that members of the above Club [St. George's] have been approached by members of the Belgrave Harriers and invited to secede from St. George's and join you, and at a meeting of my Committee I was instructed to write to you suggesting that you should request your members to refrain from such unsportsmanlike practices, and to inform you that any similar case in the future will be brought to the notice of the A.A.A. immediately.

We feel quite confident that such tactics are not approved by your Committee, and in view of the friendly relations that have hitherto existed between us I trust that you will do what you can to prevent a recurrance of the action complained of. Possibly a note in your Magazine would have the desired effect.”

From the committee minutes of January 1930 we know that our Hon. Secretary was instructed to reply to Neville and that copies of all correspondence were sent to the A.A.A. but we can't help laughing at the 70 years old pencilled reply written in the margin of the St. George's letter by Wally Bayliss.

“Dear Sir

Put a note in your own magazine advising your members not to join Belgrave as 2nd claim members.”

J. W. NevilleWally Bayliss

Report · page 10

Under pressure !

Belgrave Harriers provided the officials for a Dave Pamah/BMC/Nike spectacular at Battersea Park on June 14th 1998. There were records a-plenty as the Kenyans turned up in force to batter the season's best global marks. Patrick Ndururi headed the 800 metres field and the judges and timekeepers were severely tested as no less than seven men, most of them wearing identical Nike racing strip hit the finish line within 14 tenths! In second place James McIlroy smashed the Northern Irish record. In all there were five closely contested 800s and a couple of 1500s which were almost as tight. Further records fell in the 5,000m where South African Hendrick Ramaala set a new BMC mark of 13:33.3 and Dermot Donnelly another Northern Irish best in 2nd.

The officials, pictured left, were (from the top left to right): Gordon Biscoe (timekeeper), John Sullivan (North London timekeeper), Don Anderson (timekeeper), Yak Stegner (Herne Hill timekeeper), Terry Earle (timekeeper), Pat Mead (track referee), Stuart Bennett (track judge), Bob Bridges (timekeeper), Richard Biscoe (track judge), Alan Mead (chief timekeeper), Leslie Earle (track judge). Not pictured but recording entries and assisting with the seeding was John Jeffery while starter was Bob Bush (Serpentine).

Feature · page 10

Heroine Hilary

Great news hit the local newspapers late last summer. The headline read: “Hilary beats cancer and goes to the top of the class”.

Not only had our Hilary Saw earned a first-class degree in French, she had defied cancer at the same time as undertaking her studies. She was diagnosed with breast cancer while in the second year of her B.A. but was determined to finish her studies. She said: “I was determined not to let my world become doctors and hospitals. I am delighted to have achieved a first. I had to discipline myself to concentrate on my work, but it has all come good.

“I am no different from any other student, but it is a nice feeling to have vindicated the faith shown in me by family and friends and my tutors.”

Hilary, who previously worked as an international archivist for the Public Records Office, received chemotherapy whilst continuing her studies at Birkbeck College University in central London.

She went into remission in the final year of her course and has now become a founding member of a new cancer support group at Kingston Hospital.

She said: “ I decided to become involved to help other sufferers of the illness to get the support they need.”

It was great to see her out running in Richmond Park again on the occasion of our third men's league race.

Hilary Saw

Feature · page 11

Where are they now?

Olympian Derek Boosey, 55, recent triple jump winner at the Nike World Masters Games in Portland Oregon, where he represented Canada, must surely rank as a contender for the title 'world citizen.'

Born in India, he represented Britain at the '68 Mexico City Games, served four years in the R.A.F and attended universities in the U.S.A, England and Australia. His professional career in coaching and sports administration has not exactly kept him at 'home' either, with appointements in England (Sussex University), Australia, Saudi Arabia, Spain, Korea and Canada.

(From the Olympian magazine.)

Derek was holder of our longest survivng Club Record — a triple jump mark of 16.22m set as long ago as 1968 — until June 1998 when, 30 years and six days later, 19 year-old Phillips Idowu recorded 16.35m. (Phillips' 16.34m set in '97 was wind assisted.) Derek's leap all those years ago still keeps him in the top twenty British triple jumpers of all-time.

Is there any Belgrave Harrier from long ago that you can bring us up to date with?

Can anyone tell us of the whereabouts of our Past President Frank Simmons? We haven't heard from him in ages and have had difficulty in trying to track him down.

Derek BooseyPhillips IdowuFrank Simmons

Feature · page 11

Terror in a tree lined lane

From the South London Advertiser, "The Paper That Speaks Its Mind," January 23rd 1959.

" ... BEAST ATTACKS ACE SPORTS GIRLS! Two locals sports stars. Marianne Dew and Pam Evans of Balham were attacked by a man on Tooting Bec Common as they returned home from a training spell at Tooting Bec track.

It happened as the girls took a short cut along The Avenue, a path flanked on both sides by tall trees.

A man cycled past and muttered something. They took no notice but a few yards further on a man rushed at them. He grabbed 20 year-old Marianne by the throat. She screamed, lashed out and ran. Pam was not touched.

Later the two athletes toured the district in a police car without success.

"It all happened so quickly," Marianne told me afterwards. "He was wearing a raincoat and seemed very tall. I was terrified and Pam and I both had memories of the tow-path murder. It was all like a bad dream."

Marianne is Surrey 220 yard champion and works as a secretary in Soho.

Like Marianne, Pam is a member of Selsonia L.A.C. She was runner-up in the Surrey Cross-country. A 24 year-old teacher at Kingsdale Schools, Dulwich, she was wearing a duffle coat over her track suit at the time of the attack. "

This newspaper cutting brought much amusement to the crowd in the bar at Belgrave Hall recently as Marianne Black and Pam Davies relived the occasion forty years on. Pam, a National Cross-country champion recalls that it was the only time she ever out-sprinted Marianne!

Marianne DewPam EvansMarianne BlackPam Davies

Letter · page 11

You can't keep a good man down - our loss is Ireland's gain

Leo Coy

Leo Coy writes from County Wexford, Ireland

My life here is very different from living in London or Kent. I spend a lot of time working on the house and have also got a full time job in the local ferry terminal until Christmas.

Locally, as in Ireland in general, sport is a very important part of life. Here in Rosslare (population 2,500) there are two soccer clubs and a Gaelic football/hurling club. These clubs provide competition and training for boys and girls in age groups from eight to seniors. Schools are also keen on the promotion and participation in sport.

Athletics tends to take a back seat in comparison to other sports. In some areas it's traditionally very strong while in others it is non-existent.

At Rosslare we have a small club which caters for boys and girls aged nine to seventeen. It's called St. Paul's A.C. Competition is concentrated in the May to June period for Track & Field with all major championships finishing in July. There is also an event called Community Games which is the equivalent of the English Schools.

I have been involved in the coaching side of St. Paul's since January. Circuit training was introduced for the first time on Monday nights and, incidentally, we do the circuits in my old school class room which is now a community centre. We started with just over twenty members and this increased to nearly sixty during the summer.

Outdoor facilities are very basic with limited equipment and a grass playing field for training, supplemented by occasional runs on the beach. Cross-country was introduced to St. Paul's in September for the first time in many years and we have produced two county champions already!

It's great to be involved in athletics again and only having to concentrate on the coaching side. There are no 'phone calls or chasing people as the athletes live close by and are very enthusiastic with supportive parents.

Good luck and best wishes to everybody in Belgrave.

Leo Coy

Leo Coy

Notice · page 11

Charlie Jones on the mend

We were very sorry indeed to hear of 87 year-old Charlie Jones' bad fall last year — a fall that put him into hospital for a considerable length of time, followed by a spell at a convalescent home.

Happily, Charlie, now in his 73rd year of membership, is back at home and moving around the house pretty well with the occasional use of a frame and has recovered to such an extent that he has also recently undergone surgery to remove cataracts.

In spite of all this medical attention and recovery, however, there is nothing that our stalwart likes better than to discuss the fortunes of Belgrave Harriers and whenever a visit is made to the Jones household, the current copy of Athletics Weekly is always to be found by the side of his chair.

Charlie sends his best wishes to all Belgravians and we, Charlie, look forward to many more years of your friendship and interest in our Club.

Charlie Jones

Report · page 12

An evening with Arthur

one of our Special Correspondents

Arthur Penny's 90th Birthday and 60 years of Membership

— report by one of our Special Correspondents —

On 20th June some 70 Belgravians and friends sat down to a three course meal in Belgrave Hall to pay tribute to the Club's oldest member Arthur Penny who had recently reached both his 90th birthday and 60 years of membership. Several others who could not attend sent messages of congratulation.

The evening, organised by Gordon Biscoe and Derek Manning, proved to be a relaxed and enjoyable occasion punctuated by much laughter and merry banter. The meal was masterminded by Derek, who lived up to the high standards he has set himself both in quality and presentation. Once again Derek enlisted the aid of our President, Lionel Mann, who took time off during a busy period in his Royal duties to oversee the preparation and cooking.

Gordon in proposing a vote of thanks to them both, following the meal, explained how in the weeks leading up to the event, Derek had joined the other hooligans heading for the world cup in France, in order to bring back the wines, cheeses and fruits to grace our tables. He reminded us that Derek had spent many hours planning and scheming in order that nothing be left to chance.

Derek himself then extended a vote of thanks to all his helpers, in particular to the young Belgrave ladies who acted as waitresses, and to the bar staff.

It was at this stage in the proceedings that Gordon acquainted those present with the news that in the Queen's birthday honours list, announced that week, Lionel had been awarded the M.V.O (Member of the Victorian Order ). This was a personal award given by the Queen herself. He extended congratulations to Lionel on behalf of the Club.

With the meal completed it was then left to Gordon and Charlie Manning to revive their 'Two Ronnies Act' (first seen during the celebrations for Bill Lucas in April '97) and to introduce the formal part of the evening where it seemed that hardly anyone present went without mention!

Having threatened to get Bob Taylor to lead a round of community singing, reminiscent of his role on the old London to Brighton Relays, Gordon set the scene by welcoming the guests and old friends who had come together from far and near for the occasion.

"Who could have come further than from 'down under', " he said and bid a special welcome to Clive and Elizabeth Shippen back here visiting friends and relatives and, as he put it, "back where they belong in Belgrave Hall." Leo Coy and Susie had also made the special trip from their home in Eire as had Eric and Mavis Hall from Essex. Gordon recalled how Mavis's father, Tom Morrell, a Past President, had been a contemporary of Arthur's.

Special welcomes were also extended to Pam Davies and Dave McMillan back from severe illness and horrific injury respectively; to Dennis Brickwood our cross country captain in the 50's and still looking capable of showing many in the Club a clean pair of heels; and to special guest and friend from Herne Hill Harriers — John Gebbels and his wife Elizabeth. John is currently Chairman of the SEAA Road Running Commission and President Elect of the SEAA, very pertinent in view of Arthur's love of cross country.

Finally Gordon bid welcome to Eric Penny and his wife Pat. Apart from his athletic career in Bel's colours, Eric was truly the lynch-pin of the famous Belgrave Skiffle Group set up by Bill Couzens back in 1973. The lynch-pin because, "for a start he knew how to spell music!" Gordon further noted that, with one exception, all that group were present, i.e. Eric, Bill, Charlie Manning, Alan Mead and himself. "Now it's 25 years since we last played together," he said, "and as this is a special occasion — we're not playing tonight either! "

"We have to push on quickly," announced Gordon, "not only to let you circulate and socialise as much as possible but also to let Gerry North get to Selsey by 6-30 am tomorrow morning, where he is Director of the Selsey 10km Road Race. So he says — personally I think that as it's mid-summer's day, he's off to Stonehenge for the sunrise celebrations, dancing around with vestal virgins!"

To pay tribute to Arthur, Gordon then called upon Charlie, introduced as, "like Arthur an athlete and, like Arthur too, one who had toed the starting line of cross-country, track and road races with some of the great names of his day. Unfortunately, unlike Arthur, he was never with them at the end"

Before talking of Arthur, Charlie made a few comments of his own on those present. Everyone was advised to sit up and pay attention as the head boy (Clive Shippen) was back in town — "He's only really come back from Australia to stop me making rude comments about him as I did at the celebrations for Bill Lucas last year," Charlie further told us that, "Jim Heathfield had been waiting for the last 18 months to see Clive appear in 'Neighbours.' "

He'd also noticed that there were several present who had been in Arthur's training pack in their younger days but that Brian Gorman was not among them. "Not surprising," he observed, "for as Gordon has often said, "Brian did for pack running what the Boston Strangler did for door to door salesmen!"

Of Arthur, Charlie revealed how the archives showed him as joining the Club on the 29th February 1928 — "which means he's only been a member for 19 years! Clearly an imposter!" Well, perhaps not, as current records show the date as 28th Feb. — "or is this another 'Watergate' style cover up?" he asked.

"Arthur has never flinched from expressing his opinion strongly," he went on. "Take a typical Committee meeting. There'll be Bill Laws telling us how he has arranged with Wandsworth Council to have the inside lane at Battersea paved with gold — or Ted Pallant explaining how he has scoured the Orient for the finest silks for the next Club strip — or my brother outlining his latest scheme for a marginal increase in subs. with which he could rebuild Belgrave Hall as a five story building with neon lights, gymnasium, underground car park, restaurant, swimming pool and massage parlour. Arthur will listen to all this with riveted attention, before returning us all to earth with the succinct comment — 'I've never heard such a lot of rubbish!' "

Charlie recalled reading in the last Belgravian how our President, Lionel, had been referred to as part of the Belgrave furniture, and how this could similarly be applied to Arthur, but in his case one could take it a stage further — "He's an antique! Not because of his age, commendable in itself, but for the qualities that one looks for in an antique. You look for it to be genuine — you look for quality— you look for durability — you consider its value. To all of us present who have known him for many years we recognise he meets all these criteria."

Charlie then proceeded to give a summary of Arthur's career both as an active athlete and as an official within Belgrave (see inset) even noting that on more than one occasion he had played the role of Father Christmas at the children's parties.

"Surely ladies and gentlemen," he concluded, "with his pedigree — his service — and his loyalty, we have in Arthur Penny a piece of Belgrave furniture which is priceless. Arthur we salute you on having reached your 90th year and we honour you on your dedicated contribution to our great Club"

Not to be outdone by Charlie, Eric Hall chimed in with further anecdotes regarding Arthur. There was the one about how, having made his way by 'bus and tube to the White City where he was representing England in the Empire Games 6 miles, and where having duly broken the tape in first place he then, as a working man who had taken time off work to get there, made an enquiry about the possibility of his being reimbursed a few bob towards fares. "What," he was told by the official in charge of the purse strings. "You've got a gold medal haven't you!"

The President then proposed a toast to Arthur and, on behalf of the Club, presented him with an engraved goblet.

Having watched and listened to the whole of the evening's events with obvious enjoyment and considerable amusement, Arthur, without reference to any notes thanked all concerned for the efforts made on his behalf; thanked all those old friends who had come along to make his evening so enjoyable, and finally spoke for several minutes recalling how he came to take up athletics and the highlights of his career.

Arthur has always admitted that his first love was cross country running and from a career that started in 1928 and spanned just on twenty years we give just a sample of his many achievements:-

Cross-Country ~ • Inter Counties CC Champ. (3 times) • Southern Counties CC Champion • Six times Surrey County CC Champion • Numerous appearances in Belgrave 'National' teams • Club Champion at 7 and 10 miles

Road ~ • Belgrave's winning London-Brighton Relay team ( 6 times ) • Course record in Ilford Road Relay

Track ~ • Inter Counties 3 mile Champion (1930) • Placed in AAA 5 and 10 Mile • Winner Fire Brigade Open 3 mile (High class meeting of yesteryear ). • Empire Games 6 mile Champion 1934

As an Official ~ • Club Committee member for many years • Life member of Belgrave H • Club President 1961—62 • Member of Surrey County AA Committee • Club Starter • Junior cross-country manager

Feature · page 14

So Close to Olympic Glory

Alan Mead

Marathon Men - Etienne Gailly

Fifty years ago, at Wembley Stadium, a young Belgian with strong Belgrave affiliations came within 42 seconds of Olympic immortality by Alan Mead

The young Belgian Etienne Gailly seemed to have already packed more excitement into his twenty-five years than most of us see in a lifetime but fifty years ago this young man lined up for the 1948 Olympic Marathon with his sights set on a high placing. Just over two and a half hours later he was involved in a desperate finish that bore great similarities to the drama of that previous London Olympic Marathon of 1908 when Italian Dorando Pietri struggled to the finish in dire straits, only to be disqualified.

In 1943 at the age of twenty, with his native Belgium occupied by German forces, Etienne had left home and travelled through France with the idea of somehow reaching Britain. There he intended joining some of his free countrymen with the aim of returning to his homeland one day to help drive out the invaders. He crossed the Pyrenees but was then arrested and spent the next six months incarcerated in a Spanish gaol. In due time he was released, the intention being that he should return to Belgium but Etienne still had other ideas and his travels took him on to Portugal, Gibraltar and finally England.

Immediately he started training as a parachutist with the Brigade Belge but a tough paratrooper's training was not enough for this young man and having already done a little athletics at home he decided to join a British club. On April 29th 1944 Etienne (immediately becoming known as Steve) was elected a member of Belgrave Harriers and he found himself running alongside such notables as Carter, Cohen, Shields and Doubleday. Soon plans were being laid for the liberation of Europe. Second Lieutenant Gailly played his part and before too long was reunited with his family, although still managing to get back to see his running friends in England.

With the War over and settled back in Belgium, Etienne raced for the famous l'Union Saint-Gilloise which numbered another Belgravian among its members—top miler Bill Nankeville. His athletics progressed and by 1948 he was not only the Belgian Marathon Champion but in a match vs. the French team Stade Francais, held to celebrate the 50th anniversary of his club, Etienne lapped the field to win in 31 mins. 52 secs.— not bad for a man now considered to be a marathoner.

Entries for the marathon of the XIV Olympiad included some famous names. There was the diminutive Korean Yun Bok Soh who had set a World's Best of 2 hrs. 25 mins. 39 secs. in 1947, the Americans Ted Vogel and John Kelley, Boston winners Gérard Côte of Canada and Stylianos Kyriakides of Greece, Johannes Colemann from South Africa and a trio of Flying Finns — Viljo Heino (10,000 metre World Record breaker), Jussi Kurikkala and Mikko Hietanen (the European Champion). Britain's team included the redoubtable veteran Jack Holden, four-times winner of the International Cross Country Champs. and three times "National" champion, and the tough Welshman from South London Harriers, Tom Richards.

Wembley Stadium was full to the rafters on Saturday, August 7th, the final day of the games, as the forty-one marathon runners circled the track and were directed out of the arena by the first (and last) pointsman of the race, our own George Chantler. Of the Belgrave competitors in the Games, Bill Lucas had gone out in the heats of the 5,000 metres earlier in the week, Bill Nankeville had placed 6th in the final of the 1500 metres, run in a down-pour, and as the marathon was taking place on the roads, the 10,000 metres Walk saw a below par performance from Harry Churcher who finished 5th. Interestingly, discussions at a Club "committee meeting", held while rambling the South Downs, had resulted in an agreement that as Etienne was not now living permanently in the U.K. he would not be considered one of our "internationals" even though he was racing in the colours of Belgium; a great shame.

Under heavy skies and in humid conditions with an uncomfortable wind, the marathon field reached the Kingsbury Cross Roads and then headed on towards Stanmore. Etienne was surprised to find himself among the leaders. Together with his coach he had decided beforehand to totally ignore the other runners and follow a schedule which would bring him home in 2 hrs. 30 mins. He carried a stopwatch and regularly checked it to ensure that he was on schedule. Gradually the opposition fell away and although he was somewhat concerned that he had taken the lead so early, he decided that as he was following a schedule that he was convinced he could manage, he would stick to his plan.

The marathon route had been gradually climbing from the start and after going through Stanmore and the 6 miles point in 34 mins. 34 secs. the inclines became ever steeper as Stirling Corner and Elstree were approached. At 13 miles he held a 24 second advantage over the unknown Lou of China and over a minute on the Argentinian Guinez and Frenchman Josset. The favourites were way down the field and it began to look as if Etienne, one of the youngest men in the field, was going to bring about an almighty upset.

Unfortunately Gailly and his coach had based their calculations on a level course but the route chosen for the 1948 Marathon was later described as:

" ... one of the most punishing ever selected for the Olympic Games. Only on the track of the Empire Stadium itself did the runners get respite from the up hill and down dale of the hard metalled roads."

Etienne was coping well with the climbs but the extra energy he was expending was to cost him dear in the later stages. In his own words, reported later in World Sports magazine he tells us:

"Surprised when I saw that I was ahead of the entire field at such an early stage I was convinced the others would soon re-establish contact with me. In fact I was often glancing back, expecting Heino and Holden to appear at my side. I considered them my most dangerous rivals and it was baffling not to see them coming to the fore. ... When after about 32 kms. the Korean, Choi, passed me and I could not respond, I asked myself: 'Is he travelling so fast or am I fading?' I had to admit to myself that my strength had left me temporarily. I could only watch how fast Choi was running. Although I hated this tiredness, I was not unduly alarmed. I had been alone in the lead for 27 kms: things looked not quite so good now but, after all, I felt nothing more than a normal tiredness which I hoped to overcome soon. Next the Argentinian Cabrera passed me. This, however, did not discourage me, not even when he gained some 60 yards on me, because just at this time I felt my rhythm coming back. I had got my second—or was it my third—wind."

The Korean had run an amazing middle part of the race but no sooner was he beginning to look a likely winner than he began to limp and before long was forced to drop out of the contest. Tommy Richards was looking stronger as the race progressed and with just 2 kms. remaining it was impossible to predict the winner as just 16 seconds covered the Argentinian, the Belgian and the Welshman. Etienne takes up his story once more:

"I joined issue again ... and closed quickly ... No sooner had I got into the Argentinian's slipstream than I decided to spurt without delay. I no longer felt powerless: on the contrary, I thought that now was the time to square the account. ... I passed Cabrera, and having regained the lead, seemed to be travelling well. Certainly I was tired, but quite convinced that I would last the distance.

"It was then that I committed my biggest blunder ... To rejoin Cabrera I had to make good some 70 yards. Having achieved this I drew away from him too quickly because after about 1 km. I had left him 60 yards or so behind. This works out at over 100 metres overtake in only 2kms."

The damage was done. Etienne was terribly exhausted—far more so than he realised. He still led as the stadium was approached but was mercilessly exposed to the two men doggedly trailing him. Sheridan tells us:

"... with only a few hundred yards remaining it became apparent that all was not well ... Etienne Gailly was in obvious distress. Ashen faced with eyes bulging, he lost his sureness of stride and was almost staggering. Was he to undergo the same fate as had befallen Dorando Pietri forty years earlier?"

And Etienne again:

"I had no premonition of collapsing. At the very moment, however, when I stepped on the track, exhaustion overcame me like a powerful drug. Immediately after I had crossed the normal finishing line—unfortunately this was not the real finish—this lay 400 metres further on—I knew that I was going to be passed. I cannot deny that this last lap was hard for me. It was like the progress of a martyr. I was horribly weak, indeed, I almost fainted ... First Cabrera and then Richards passed me, as behind a veil. I was no longer fighting them but that awful engulfing weakness, wanting more than anything to get to that unbelievably distant finishing line. I got there ... somehow."

The previously unrated Cabrera had run a perfectly judged race and seemed so strong at the end in comparison to the tottering Gailly. Afterwards he revealed that he ran no more than 36 miles a week in training. Thrilled as the spectators were with a British silver medal for Richards, Gailly's heroic struggle endeared him to the crowd and to all the athletics writers of the day. Within a week he had recovered enough to take part in a 3,000 metre race in Belgium and then he was 2nd in an inter-Allied Games 5,000 metres held in Antwerp. By September, in Prague, he was on international duty for Belgium again as he ran to 2nd in the 10,000 metres vs. Czechoslovakia.

What a man. In Belgium he was awarded the Grand Prix d'honneur as the outstanding athlete of the year and the Trophie National du Merite Sportif for his performance in London. With youth on his side he surely would have been a red hot favourite for the 1952 Olympic Games in Helsinki and then there was 1956 and even 1960 when he would still be of an age similar to many of the pre-race favourites in the London event—but it didn't happen. Lieutenant Gailly was, after all, a soldier first and foremost and his adventures went on. In November 1951, while with his regiment of paratroopers in Korea, he was wounded when an exploding mine shattered seven bones in his left foot. He was flown to Tokyo for hospital treatment and later returned to Belgium for convalescence before his anticipated return to South East Asia for further duty. He was expected to have a permanent limp for the rest of his life and, as far as we know, Etienne never competed again.

Sources: • Good Reasons: 100 years of Olympic Marathon Races by Michael Sheridan; published by the author 1996, ISBN 0952819201, obtainable from the author at [address withheld]. • World Sports Magazine 1948 and 1952. • The Belgravian, 1948, 1949. • Athletics, 1948. • The Miracle of the Mile, Bill Nankeville, Stanley Paul 1956. • Olympic Games, Tom McNab, Knight Books 1971, ISBN 0340150637. • Conversations with Charlie Jones.
