Feature · page 16

Newsman who will be a winner in the long run

David Powell

Marathon Men - Dave McMillan

This article appeared in The Times of April 20 1998, just prior to Dave McMillan's return to the streets of London where he covered the 26.2 miles in 4 hrs.23 mins.02 secs.

David Powell on the club athlete whose dream of sporting glory ended when he almost died after a horrific accident

Dave McMillan was an ordinary club runner with the chance to achieve celebrity status. It might have taken him another 25 years or more, but he was determined to persevere.

The first sign of interest in his one shot at sporting glory came in a letter from The Times. This newspaper was seeking information on the 42 runners — all men — who had completed every London Marathon and wanted to know how many would be lining up for No 16 that spring.

McMillan's reply was dated January 31, 1996. In it, he said: "I'll run London again, I wouldn't miss it for the — world." Well, he did miss it, and got nothing in return, except a story about the day that running almost cost him his life.

Only four days after penning his reply to The Times, McMillan was involved in an horrific road accident while out running. So serious were the injuries from his collision with a car that the paramedic in the ambulance decided that his patient would die before reaching hospital. The only hope was to pull into a lay-by and perform emergency surgery. Even then, McMillan's chances were slim. The operation, a needle thoracotomy, had a track record of 100 per cent failure. "Three paramedics before mine all lost their patients," McMillan said. "Mine was the first successfully to carry it out."

McMillan had been found with fractures to his right arm, right leg, ribs and shoulder, but it was not until the ambulance was on its way to hospital that Graham White, the paramedic, became aware of extensive internal injuries. White discovered that oxygen was not getting through to the blood and that McMillan's right lung was collapsing.

Dave McMillan had his right leg broken in four places, bone smashed in his right arm and nerve almost severed, five fractured ribs, shoulder blade broken in two places, and a punctured lung

"He had to pierce through the chest wall into the lung with a steel syringe to drain the lung, which was filling up with blood," McMillan said. "The paramedic only has to be a quarter of an inch out and the patient has had it. Graham was voted Paramedic of the Year for saving my life."

McMillan spent four days in intensive care, a month in hospital and returned for further surgery while the sixteenth London Marathon was taking place. Having never missed the race, he was now forced to watch from a hospital bed.

"I wanted to get up and run it," McMillan said, recalling his frustration. "I thought that, if I could keep on doing the London Marathon, I might eventually be the only person who has run them all. Once I saw that race going ahead without me, I knew I had lost that chance."

McMillan's instinct as a journalist told him that the last person left with a 100 per cent London record would attract big media attention. It may be another 25 years or more, given that the youngest is still only 39, but it meant everything to him to remain a contestant. Those who have kept going are showing a reluctance to fall by the wayside. Of the 42 ever-presents who ran in 1995, all but McMillan lined up in 1996 and 1997. Being a journalist, McMillan also understands the interest in him now. On April 26, he will be back, for his first marathon since his accident. "I cannot wait," he said. "It is going to feel fantastic."

McMillan, a Belgrave Harrier, uses running as a vent for the pressures of work. In 15 London Marathons, he has finished between three and four hours every time. When he was out for a 14-mile run that eventful January morning in 1996, his mind was set on breaking three hours for the first time, having run 3 hrs. 5 mins. the year before.

The competitor in him remains. To finish would be remarkable, but McMillan, 50, has higher goals. "In the 15 I have run, I have never been over four hours. My aim is to keep under four hours. When I applied to run in the first race, I had never run a marathon, but I finished in 3:42. My slowest is 3:56 in 1991. "Though he has set sub-four hours as his target, he is taking nothing for granted. How can he when he recalls that it was 11 months before he was allowed to walk and 20 before he returned to running last October. "The greatest fear every time I go out is that the leg will give way," McMillan said.

However, he has the added incentive of using the marathon as a vehicle to raise money for the trauma unit at the John Radcliffe Hospital, Oxford, where he arrived after his roadside surgery. McMillan's record of never having missed a London Marathon may have disappeared into the back of an ambulance, but at least others may now benefit from his will to return.

Dave McMillanDavid PowellGraham WhiteMike Nicholson
See it on the printed page →
Feature · page 17

Belgrave Stars of the Silver Screen

John Jeffery

— John Jeffery writes —

An interesting side-line of being members of a large and successful club like Belgrave is that we are often asked to provide athletes for advertisements and sports programmes.

So it was, that on a cold and gloomy day at Crystal Palace, half a dozen Belgravians gathered to compete in a 1500 metres race which would be filmed and later converted, using computer technology, into an interactive entertainment for youngsters and budding athletes. It would be initially shown in Munich at the International Museum of Sport and subsequently sent to various Sports Academies around the World.

Briefly, the idea was that a motor-bike with a cameraman on board would film the race as if he were one of the competitors, finishing in 3rd or 4th position, and with careful editing it would appear to be a genuine race.

And so they lined up! Wearing assorted vests, including some Belgrave ones, we had Matt Yates, Junior Galley, Clive Gilby, Dave Pamah and Kevin Quinn. To add to the international theme I twisted John Bicourt's arm and he supplied three of his Kenyan all-stars.

The gun went and the race began. The cameraman was left at the start and had to spend the first lap weaving through the field to catch up. Matt Yates broke the habit of a lifetime and was up with the leader Clive Gilby. As the race unfolded various athletes took the lead until coming off the final bend, Matt broke away and held off the Kenyans to win in 3 mins. 54 secs. If only real life was as easy!

The Director of the film was impressed with the initial results and it only remained for he and I to review the action and release the athletes.

The first lap looked great with a group of athletes fighting for the lead in typical middle-distance style. And then, in growing horror, we saw that emerging from the corner of the screen was the lovely Mrs. Gilby (Clive's Mum) pushing his little boy into the second lane to cheer Daddy round the track.

The Director turned several shades of puce before frantically using all his expertise to work out how to delete Grandmother, baby and pushchair from the action. We went into hasty conference. There was only one answer, we would have to do it again.

I was delegated to break the good news to the athletes who were already warming down. Being loyal and true Belgravians they were all very phlegmatic and reconciled to the situation, treating the thought of running again with jovial banter. Did I really write that! No, let's be truthful, they went ballistic. There were immediate threats that included water jump, Mrs. Gilby, baby, pushchair and finally even the innocent Clive.

Eventually we calmed them down and after much deliberation and careful scripting commenced the strangest 1500 metre race in history. Half the athletes started the race followed by the cameraman and bike. They ran the first lap and were then replaced by the rest of the field who were hiding at the start of the first bend. This went on for 3 laps with athletes filling in and dropping out until more by luck than judgement we had enough athletes on their feet to finish the race.

Apart from two athletes (who shall remain nameless) being unable to catch up at any stage, it all worked very well and any of you visiting the Munich Museum will have the chance to peruse the finished product. If that is too far to go, the producer is sending me a copy which I will be delighted to lend to interested parties. Unfortunately I have been unable to persuade Mrs. Gilby to sign a copy for me.

Who won the race? I'm sorry you asked that as, to make up the numbers, we brought in Mark Death who is one of the Senior Managers at Crystal Palace and a middle-distance runner. In the scramble for the line he dipped first. What's wrong with that I hear you ask?

He's a member of Blackheath!

John JefferyMatt YatesJunior GalleyClive GilbyDave PamahKevin QuinnJohn BicourtMrs. GilbyMark Death

Feature · page 17

Cynthia James - Life Member of Belgrave Harriers

In 1983 the James family first came into contact with Belgrave Harriers when 12 year old Cindy joined our women's team. Cindy brought with her more than her own athletic talent for within a very short space of time her mum and dad, Cynthia and Raymond, were helping out at track and field meetings and as time went by even young brother Richard found himself racing in Belgrave colours.

Now, fifteen years later we can look back on the very valued help and friendship that this family has brought to our club, but in particular it is time that we officially recognised the work that has been put in by Cynthia.

It was no time at all before Cynthia began to assist our Women's Team Manager, taking on ever more of the pre-match 'phone calls and hassles; ensuring that the girls got to their starting marks in time for their events; stepping in herself to heave a shot, discus or hammer should the need arise. She officially became Joint Team Manager with Harry Porter and continues in that role today with Jacqui Smiter.

But Cynthia hasn't confined herself to looking after her athletes and chasing about with her team sheet. Countless times a thirsty track or field judge has had cause to thank her as she has provided seemingly unlimited supplies of tea, coffee and a veritable haystack of fruit cake and biscuits. It would be true to say that, thanks to Cynthia, we are now regarded as a club that always "look after their officials". It was not always so.

Remember that all this team managing is not just for the Women's Southern League Team but for our Thameside League as well. And Cynthia has not confined her role to one of helping out the women's team only. Our men's matches always run better for Cynthia's presence and she soon has the lads carrying refreshments around – as meek as any lambs.

None of this is a once a week activity either. A whole Saturday can be spent organising her team in the driving rain in Huntingdonshire or Cornwall; the whole of the following Sunday might be spent competing for our Veterans' team at Colindale, while Wednesday night might involve catering for the Rosenheim officials and Friday night a Track & Field Committee Meeting ... it goes on and on.

Cynthia JamesCindy JamesRaymond JamesRichard JamesHarry PorterJacqui Smiter

Report · page 18

Gold & Silver at the Nepstadion

Track & Field - European Championships - Budapest

— The 17th European Championships in Athletics, Budapest, Hungary, 18-23 August 1998 —

What a superb arena the Nepstadion is. Plans for a stadium on this site were originally drawn up when Budapest was asked to host the first Modern Olympic Games in 1896. As it turned out those games eventually went to Greece and what was to become the chief sports facility of Hungary was not begun until 1948 with completion in 1953. Since then some outstanding events have been hosted: Davis Cup Tennis, European Basketball, many international Soccer matches including some graced by the legendary Puskas. Evangelist Billy Graham and Pope John Paul II have both filled the stadium—as did rock bands Queen and Genesis—but athletics has been far from overshadowed with no less than 23 World Records being set here.

One thousand four hundred and ninety four athletes from 47 countries gathered together in this inspiring environment to compete in the 17th European Championships in Athletics—including eight Belgravians.

100m ~ Darren Campbell (Gold) and Dwain Chambers (Silver)

As heats progressed to second rounds and then to semi finals, the 100 metres always looked like being a two horse race—and it still looked that way with 30-40 metres of the final gone. Then came Darren Campbell's electrifying finish that swept him away from his younger rival and across the line for a time four hundredths of a second faster than his coach Linford Christie's previous Championship Best and two hundredths faster than Dwain Chamber's Club Record.

There had been many who thought that Dwain might have pulled it off himself. His starting was tremendous and his aggression during each race was plain for all to see, but at only 20 years of age he will surely collect further medals in the event. He's also proved that he's got the right temperament and that his World Junior Record of 10.06 from 1997 was far from being a fluke.

It was the first ever British 1-2 in a European 100m final—and both of them Belgrave boys too! Tremendous.

Round 1, 18Aug., 6 hts, q = first 4 + 8 fastest losers: ht4 (-0.3) 1, Campbell 10.40; ht5 (+0.6) 2, Chambers 10.28.

Round 2, 18Aug., 4 hts, q = first 4: ht2 (-0.1) 1, Campbell 10.26; ht3 (-0.2) 1, Chambers 10.26.

Semi-finals, 19Aug., q = first 4: sf1 (+0.3) 1, Chambers 10.15; sf2 (+0.5) 1, Campbell 10.11.

Final, 19Aug.: (+0.3) 1, D.Campbell 10.04; 2, D.Chambers 10.10; 3, H.Papadias (Greece) 10.17.

1500m ~ Matt Yates

It seemed a different Matt Yates in Budapest. We've seen him qualify for other games and then simply not do himself justice but this time he seemed so positive, qualifying confidently and then lining up with the other Britons Whiteman and Mayock to tackle the Iberian onslaught. Always right in the bunch, he was still prepared to charge at the medal positions as the final lap unwound but the early pace had been just right for the 'sprinters' and Estevez, Silva and Cacho were moving too fast for any ground to be regained over the final 200 metres. In 6th place (3rd Briton) Matt just failed to equal John Gladwin's 5th place from 1986.

Round 1, 18Aug., 2 hts, q = first 4 + 4: ht2 3, 3:38.97.

Final, 20Aug.: 1, R.Esteves (Spain) 3:41.31; 2, R.Silva (Portugal) 3:41.84; 3, F.Cacho (Spain) 3:42.13; 6, M.Yates 3:42.63.

110m Hurdles ~ Andy Tulloch

Andy had been struggling with injury problems in the months before the games, only having raced once in seven weeks. To qualify for the semi-final with 13.81 and then improve again into a 2 metre wind was an honourable performance. Roll on the Commonwealth Games ...

Round 1, 18Aug., 4 hts, q = first 3 + 4: ht2 (?.?) 3, 13.81.

Semi-finals, 22Aug., q = first 4: sf2 (-2.0) 7, 13.79.

400m Hurdles ~ Chris Rawlinson

In spite of an early season 49.81, 1998 has seen Chris Rawlinson only a shadow of the man who ran 49.69 the previous year to qualify for the World Champs. He must have been extremely disappointed with 7th place in his heat in Budapest and in fact this turned out to be an altogether lack-lustre event for Britain as both our other representatives failed to progress in similar fashion.

Round 1, 18Aug., 4 hts, q = first 3 + 4: ht2 7, 51.11.

High Jump ~ Brendan Reilly

Twelve jumpers separated themselves from the non-qualifiers by clearing 2.24m, our own Brendan Reilly needing all three attempts to make the cut. Come the final and the Belgrave man oozed confidence as he ignored 2.15 and 2.20 and became the last competitor to open his account, sailing over 2.24m at his very first try. Missing 2.27m, Bren's next attempt was at 2.30 but two tries here while six other men cleared, showed that it was going to have to be all or nothing and in spite of having a season's best of 'only' 2.28 he took his final leap with the bar at 2.32. It wasn't to be. A clearance would have put him in the top five jumpers and shooting for a medal but nevertheless it was a good competitive performance from a man who only qualified for the games on the last possible day.

Qualifying rounds, 19Aug., q = 2.27 or best 12: group B 5, 2.24 (2.20xo, 2.24xxo).

Final 21Aug.: 1, A.Partyka (Poland) 2.34; 2, D.Grant (GB&NI) 2.34; 3, S.Klyugin (Russia) 2.32; ... =9, B.Reilly 2.24 (2.24o, 2.30xx-, 2.32x).

Discus ~ Nick Sweeney

A repeat of his 64.28m set in Helsingborg, Sweden, in July would not only have seen Nick through the qualifying round, it would have taken him to 5th place in the final. Two no throws came from our big Irishman, putting huge pressure on him, while Schult and Dubrovschik both reached out to 63 metres plus. The Belgrave man pulled himself together on his last attempt and got himself up to 8th in his pool with 60.36 but when the second pool had completed their throws it became apparent that our man had missed qualifying by a mere 23cms. Only three throwers had landed their platters beyond the qualifying arc, with Nick being an unlucky 13th overall and 12 being taken through to the final.

Qualifying rounds, 22Aug., q = 62.50 or best 12: group A 8, 60.36 (x, x, 60.36).

4x100m Relay ~ Darren Campbell and Dwain Chambers (Gold)

With five out of six sprint medals having gone to Britain, nobody doubted the ability of the GB boys to bring home another gold. The heats underlined that fact. A steady run, with Darren Campbell on his customary leg down the back straight and Dwain Chambers cruising the home stretch, clearly gave Britain the fastest qualifying time. Incredibly, when it came to the final, Devonish and Chambers were dropped in favour of a last pairing of 200m victor and bronze medallist Doug Walker and Julian Golding. Whilst not necessarily arguing about the inclusion of the latter two, one had to question the selectors' reasoning behind leaving Condon on leg one—a man a full 3.5 metres slower than Chambers. Apparently they wanted the same team that had run in the European Cup. British coach and selector Richard Simmons remained tight-lipped when being questioned while Dwain seemed mystified and just shrugged his shoulders. It made no difference to the result but the British winning time was slower than that of their heat and a chance of a 37 clocking was lost.

Round 1, 22Aug., 2 hts, q = first 3 + 2: ht1 1, GB&NI 38.47 (Condon, CAMPBELL, Devonish, CHAMBERS).

Final, 23Aug.: 1, 38.52 (Condon, CAMPBELL, Walker, Golding); 2, France 38.87; 3, Poland 38.98.

4x400m Relay ~ Sean Baldock (Gold)

After waiting in the wings all week for his chance to step out onto the Nepstadion track, Sean Baldock ran superbly with his second leg run in the heats. A 45.5 clocking made him second fastest of the quartet but with top men Iwan Thomas and Mark Richardson taking a breather before the final, no-one had any complaints about the final selection on this occasion. Well done Sean.

Round 1, 22Aug., 2 hts, q = first 3 + 2: ht2 1, GB&NI 3:02.37 (Hylton 45.7, BALDOCK 45.5, Wariso 46.1, Baulch 45.05).

Final, 23Aug.: 1, GB&NI (M.Hylton, J.Baulch, I.Thomas, M.Richardson) 2:58.68; 2 Poland 2:58.88; 3, France 3:02.38.

Report · page 19

10.03 from Dwain Chambers!

IAAF WORLD CUP JOHANNESBURG, RSA, 11-13 SEP 1998

As European Cup victors the British men took their place in the World Cup final alongside teams representing six continents, USA and Germany, slotting in this meet between European and Commonwealth Games. The first day's competition saw Dwain Chambers take full advantage of the 1748m altitude. A sensational start had Dwain in the lead with Africa's Ogunkoya but recovering from a poor getaway, Barbadian Obadele Thompson, racing for the Americas, rocketed through to one of the sprint performances of the year 69.87. Dwain's time in 3rd was the second fastest ever by a Briton, making him once again the fastest Belgravian of all time; but the altitude wasn't the only helpful factor. His reaction time at the start was revealed as 0.063 when 0.100 is the fastest permitted, anything less being judged as an anticipated start and therefore illegal. The starter chose not to recall the athletes so the result stood but if, for comparison's sake, we adjust his reaction time to his more normal 0.130 we come up with something like 10.10. The National Union of Track Statisticians have taken his 10.10 from Budapest as his season's best with this 10.03 being classed as a "rolling start"; we must follow suit.

Another to enjoy the thin air was Mike Edwards, called up for the pole vault on day two. First time clearances came at 5.00 and 5.20 while two attempts were needed for 5.40. A massive 5.60 was the next height to be attempted and with a best ever of 5.52 dating from 1993 it was not surprising to find that Mike had reached his limit.

The hammer event saw a psyched up Mick Jones blasting it from round one. As he later said, it wasn't until he calmed down and concentrated that he managed to start putting his hammer out where it should have been going. He was pleased to put South Africa's Chris Harmse behind him though. Harmse was one of his main rivals for the following week's Commonwealth Games.

Further great running came from Chambers as he anchored the GB team to a winning 38.09 in the 4x100 metres-a Championship Best-and Sean Baldock continued his tremendous season with a 45.1 leg in the long relay.

a=11th, b=12th, c=13th. 100m a: (-0.2) 3, D.Chambers 10.03. PV b: 6, M.Edwards 5.40 (5.00o, 5.20o, 5.40xo, 5.60xxx). HT b: 5, M.Jones 72.89 (x, 70.86, x, x, 72.89, 70.10). 4x100m b: 1, GB&NI (A.Condon, M.Devonish, J.Golding, D.Chambers) 38.09. 4x400m c: 2, GB&NI (M.Hylton 45.8, J.Baulch 44.7, S.BALDOCK 45.1, I.Thomas 44.1).

Feature · page 19

Nick Sweeney sets another National Discus Record

Above: Tremendous discus throwing came from Nick Sweeney throughout the summer of '98 and Helsinborg, Sweden, almost became a second home to the 30 year-old Irishman. Standing 1.95m tall and weighing in at 121kg, Nick previously had a best of 67.40 dating from 1996.

Having travelled to the Swedish town to spin his platter out to 64.28 in July, he was back in August, this time reaching out to 65.59. Not at his best in Budapest for the European Games, Nick barely scraped over 60m but on September 4th, once more in Helsingborg, he launched a mighty 67.89 for his eighth Irish Record - a distance 2 metres in excess of the British Record. Two days later at the same venue he set his second best mark of the season with 65.70.

Nick is a graduate of Harvard University and played Rugby Union for Irish Schools.

Nick SweeneyAlan Mead

Report · page 21

Fifteen at the Friendly Games

Commonwealth Games - Kuala Lumpur - Track & Field

Fifteen at the Friendly Games

Commonwealth Games, Kuala Lumpur, Malaysia, 16-21 September

Late September, and at the "Friendly Games" a long athletics season drew to a close with some explosive performances. The sprints lacked only the Americans while the middle-distances were dominated by Kenyans. An unprecedented fifteen Belgravians made the long trip to the national capital of Malaysia, braving warnings of crippling smog and excessive humidity and temperatures to compete in the Bukit Jalil stadium.

Gold and silver came our way in the relays and that old war-horse Regis came up with a bronze medal but this time it was the field events that gave us our closest shots at individual gold.

A medal at last for Mick Jones

For Mick Jones Kuala Lumpur was a dream come true. Twice before in Commonwealth Games competition he had finished just out of the medals in fourth place but in 1998 there was to be no mistake. The South African Chris Harmse led after round one, his 72.83 edging out Australian Stuart Rendell's 72.52 and Mick's 72.58. Dave Smith's 67.31 placed him 5th. But while the South African had launched his "big one", round two saw Rendell improve to 74.37 and Mick hanging on to silver medal position with 73.51. A lifetime personal best came from the Belgrave man in round 3—74.02 while Rendell fouled. That was it as far as the medals were concerned. The Australian backed up his leading mark with 74.33 for his fourth effort and then finished off with a winning 74.71for his sixth throw.

Dave Smith heaved his best of the competition in round four to go back into 5th. Still only 24 he must have as good a chance as anyone to pick up that elusive gold in some future Games but he'll still have that man Jones to deal with for a while yet. For the first time in his life 35 year-old Mick is to go warm weather training this winter, travelling to South Africa with his coach Alan Bertram. If he can throw 74.02 after training in a Dorking field, thanks to a helpful farmer, the best could yet be ahead of him.

Silver for Ben Challenger

That man Dalton Grant and our own Ben Challenger were the men leading the way in the high jump. Dalton came in at 2.15 with Ben delaying until 2.20—both had first time clearances and then it was up to 2.24 where the pair went clear again, as did Brendan Reilly, while Australia's Forsyth, Lendis of South Africa and Naiko of Mauritius all needed two shots. Now it was 2.28 where Grant and Challenger continued their first time successes, this time taking Forsyth with them. Naiko cleared the bar on his third try while Brendan failed twice and saved his last attempt for 2.31.

This was where it would be settled. Grant has a reputation for being a "big-time" jumper and he did it again. The bar was repeatedly rattled off the pegs by all remaining athletes until with his third attempt the Haringey man wriggled his way over to become the only man to go clear. Ben's silver medal was a terrific achievement. He had injured an ankle while competing in the European Cup and had missed out on Budapest in an effort to get himself into shape for this competition—and he was within an ace of carrying off the gold.

Jason Lobo and Dave Pamah kept us represented in the middle-distance events. Jason was only just outside his season's best in placing 6th in his semi, while Dave, racing for his mother's St. Kitts & The Grenadines, started off a flurry of e-mails between the IAAF and The Belgravian "offices" as it became apparent that as a representative of those islands his performances of recent years made him national record holder for the 800 and 1,500 metres!

Over the barriers, a lifetime best came from Northern Ireland's Matt Douglas in the one-lap race and Andy Tulloch continued his comeback from injury with a super 13.57, his best of the year, to reach the final where he placed 6th.

Discus man Lee Newman took advantage of Welsh ancestry to take part in the discus competition. His season's best would have given him a fighting chance of 4th place but at 7th he nevertheless got the satisfaction of putting one over on British Record holder Perris Wilkins.

For Ian Tullett it was not a happy time at all as he sufferred the ignominy of three fouls at his opening height in the pole vault.

Roller-coaster ride for Ladejo

In only his second full Decathlon, Du'aine Ladejo's 7th place has got to be considered a success but he will be far from satisfied with his performance. A near pb came in the 100 metres to take him straight to the top of the leaders board—and then came an awful long jump where two fouls nearly saw him exiting the competition before he popped out a mediocre but safe 6.92. The shot was passable but then the high jump resulted in a super pb of 2.04 and a season's best of 46.12 in the 400 metres rounded off day one with the Belgrave man lying in 3rd place on 4317 points. — And then came day two and the hurdles. How Du'aine got over the first flight after totally messing up his start we'll never know. Amazingly he still registered 14.91 but it could have been so much faster—and the rest of the competition was a mere recovery exercise. Putting it all together in just two days is going to be a real challenge but some of his performances are awesome and those in the know tell us that Du'aine is sure to register 8,000 points soon.

Gold and Silver in the relays

Again we were well represented in the sprints but Euro-Champ Darren Campbell, although reaching the final and consolidating on his new level of performance, had to give way to the might of Bolden, Fredericks & Co. Dwain Chambers was unlucky to get only 5th in his semi-final with a time the same as Campbell's. The 200 metres saw 'JR' back in the big-time following an achilles tendon injury. John Regis, a silver medallist at each of the preceding Commonwealth 200s was delighted with his bronze medal and in spite of 33 years now showing on the clock has vowed to go on until 2000!

We had to wait for the relays for sprint gold where in the one lap race England romped to a Championship Best Performance of 38.20 with Dwain Chambers providing the start and Darren Campbell the glory leg. Sean Baldock contributed to the 4x400 squad's 'silver' by opening up for England in the heats but then missed the final again as the team was switched around. With so many talented one lap runners in this country he's going to have to wait a bit longer before he can get into the final four.

Report · page 22

Nightmare to Europe

Bill Laws

Track & Field - European Champion Clubs Cup

Nightmare to Europe

— Bill Laws writes —

The Journey

The 17 hour trip from Heathrow to Belgrade was the final depressing stage of a planning nightmare. It all started well back in November '97 when we were informed the ECCC Group 'B' match was to be held in Belgrade on Saturday 30th May 1998.

A quick check with WGT management, BAF's recommended travel group, showed that out of Gatwick with BA on the Friday and back to Gatwick on the Sunday was the easiest route. Great, we booked 36 seats at a special rate (and I mean special!) and in February we wrote to all our British League team giving them advance notice.

As these letters were being delivered we received an urgent fax from PARTIZAN AC the host club. Due to a Yugoslav vs. Nigeria football match scheduled also for 30th May, could we agree to putting the match back to Sunday 31st May when it would be televised. "No trouble" we faxed, "if you pay for an extra night's accommodation." No problem was their reply, so back to WGT requesting the return flight be changed to Monday 1st June – at no extra cost. "Done", faxed Ian Dennis from WGT and again we sat back and relaxed.

Towards the end of April we received a welcoming fax from the British Embassy in Belgrade requesting travel details. On receipt of our reply, HM Consul Bob Gordon phoned to say that he had good information that there was no BA flight to Belgrade on the Friday. We immediately checked with the World's Favourite Airline who informed us that although there was no Friday flight at present, it would be in the summer schedule due to start mid-May. This information was duly passed back to the British Embassy who was adamant that there would be no agreement to the Friday flight. They were right. Two weeks before departure BA informed us that we would need to take Thursday's flight out of Gatwick as the Friday one had not been introduced. Surprise, surprise! Things were getting out of hand. We were now looking at a 5 day trip for a 1 day match, more hotel costs and the athletes needing to take another day off work.

What was the alternative? BA suggested a flight to Budapest on the Friday, which would then involve a 4 hour coach drive to Belgrade. OK but they would have to pay for the coach. BA refused this suggestion but a further reduction in ticket price kept us off the boil. Only just.

WGT re-arranged tickets and organised the coach through Budatours in Budapest. Great, our troubles were over. Well, not quite. The day before departure (Thursday) Eurotours refused to accept VISA payment (we deduced this was because BAF was in Administration and had the credit rating of a block of concrete!) They wanted cash, 865 GBP to be precise. No cash, so 'your's truly' dashed to the bank, loaded up with a wad of the folding stuff and prepared for the next disaster.

As a result of these changes, Chris Rawlinson was forced to withdraw due to an exam at Loughborough on Monday 1st June. Meanwhile, for those who remembered to bring passports to the first BAL match, we (John Jeffery) made four journeys to the Yugoslav Embassy in London. Others sent passports by post and by the time we had got all the Visa's, we knew our way about Kensington quite well. We had heard that the Yugoslav's were turning round anyone without Visa's, so the airlines were checking carefully all documents. The last problem came with the late replacement of Brendan Reilly by Ben Challenger. We flew Ben out JAL on the Saturday without a Visa. Fortunately, we had enough time having arrived a day earlier to ease the release of Ben's Visa and our minder – Marina – was proving a tower of strength.

But back to the coach trip from Budapest to Belgrade. Scheduled for 4 hours, it eventually took 7 hours including a lengthy stop at the Hungarian–Yugoslavian border. We came to the conclusion that the drivers were on an hourly rate because they would not take the coach over 50mph even on a clear dual carriageway. Attempts to give them financial inducements failed and the frustration of being overtaken uphill by a fully laden cement lorry needs to be experienced to be appreciated. The team took it all in good spirits but the effects of a 17 hour trip on their bodies was to become apparent 36 hours later.

The Finances

In previous years we have managed to defray most costs of the ECCC matches by donations from the British League, BAF and the Sports & Arts Foundation. This year the League, although without a sponsor, donated £2,000, BAF couldn't and the Sports & Arts Foundation informed us that due to the Lottery creaming off football pools income, they too could not subscribe.

At one stage, the Belgrave Committee decreed that even though we were the UK Clubs' representatives, it would not be possible for Belgrave to contribute from its own stretched budget. Withdrawal was a real possibility until our Patron and No.1 supporter, Sir Eddie, stepped in to meet the substantial outstanding balance. Sir Eddie travelled to Belgrade via Rome and once again gave the team tremendous support. We hope to repay him in 1999 with promotion to Group 'A' in Europe. As I write, it appears that the venue will be Athens. Let us hope the travel arrangements will be a lot easier. I couldn't face another fiasco so soon after the Belgrade trip!

Bill LawsIan DennisBob GordonChris RawlinsonJohn JefferyBrendan ReillyBen ChallengerMarinaSir EddieMick Jones

Feature · page 22

Most ambitious year yet

Following last year's double victory in both the Cup and League Division One, our '98 targets included not only a repeat of the Golden Double but also the European Clubs Champions Cup Final to be held in Belgrade at the end of June. In 1997 Sale had been last in the 'A' Cup match and so the Great Britain representative team was now to compete in the 'B' Match. Promotion was on our mind. Because at our last appearance in 'A' Group two years ago in Istanbul we had finished in a creditable bronze position, we thought that promotion would be a formality. Oh boy, were we wrong!

Report · page 23

A jaded team ends up 4th in Belgrade

Bill Laws

European Champion Clubs Cup - Track & Field

A jaded team ends up 4th in Belgrade

EUROPEAN CLUBS CUP DIVISION 2 BELGRADE, YUGOSLAVIA 31 MAY 1998

Last year Sale were relegated in a tough 'A' Group final held in Moscow. Although not at their strongest Sale had reported that several central European teams had turned out national squads and the Russian contingent had trounced Larios who in previous years had proved invincible. This should have been a warning to us but conscious that we had finished 3rd in the 'A' Group final in '96, we planned for our visit to Belgrade content in the belief that it would be our honour to return a GB team back to the top level of European Club Competition. Nothing short of a win would be satisfactory.

Team selection was to take place after the first British League Match and it was only following the season's opener at Watford, that we began to realise we might not have the strongest of teams for Belgrade.

In the throws, Matt Simpson reported that his elbow injury needed surgery and the operation was planned for 1st June. Nick Sweeney, also based in the USA, had had surgery and was throwing discus comparable distances to both Lee Newman and Kevin Brown. There were rumours that javelin stalwart Nigel Bevan had moved to Birchfield, although he was still in the USA and was uncontactable and in the hammer Dave Smith's recovery from a broken ankle had not moved to a point where he could challenge a resurgent Mick Jones.

The middle distance was presenting problems too, as Matt Yates reported that a warm weather training injury had not recovered. Tom Lerwill was having a year's break to recover from glandular fever and Paul Evans was still laid low from problems incurred during an extended overseas training trip prior to the London Marathon. Fortunately, Jason Lobo was eligible and although specialising at 800 we chose to bring Matt Kloiber for the 800 and move Jason up to 1500. New member James Ellis Smith was selected for the 5000, not because of his 3.42 1500m pb, but because he had wintered in South Africa and had some good road race results under his belt. With Justin Chaston tied by work commitments in the USA, Lee Hurst was to prove a valuable choice for the steeplechase.

No problems with the jumps squad until a week before departure, when Brendan Reilly was injured in a car crash and there was a last minute panic to get a visa for welcome newcomer Ben Challenger, who was now destined to make his début in a Belgrave vest in the high jump in Belgrade.

The sprints squad was strong with only Dwain Chambers unavailable due to a number of overseas engagements. Clearly the world junior 100m record holder was of interest to a number of early season meeting promoters.

With hindsight it is difficult to comprehend why the ECCC committee chose a venue like Belgrade. You will read elsewhere of the travel nightmare. Suffice to say that on the day, a somewhat jaded team performed adequately but without much inspiration. What was achieved was outstanding under the circumstances with a few excellent performances. The experience and professionalism of what, after all, was a very young team, showed through and we all left a lot wiser vowing we would be better prepared for the '99 event should we be fortunate enough to qualify to represent the GB clubs again.

Unlike the 'A' Group event, the 'B' Group was to complete all events in a single day. The 10,000m was removed from the 'B' event and the Yugoslavs, mindful of the publicity value of such an important event, had placed 16 TV cameras at the stadium and the match was televised and broadcast over a 3 hour period. Central Europe received the satellite TV programme.

It was not until Saturday evening's Technical Meeting that we realised how strong the other Clubs were. The local team Partizan comprised a full Yugoslav squad and our interpreter, Marina, would not contemplate anything other than their victory. She would like it if Belgrave came second. As it turned out, so would we. Sporting Club of Portugal had a number of overseas members including former World Junior Sprint Champion Francis Obikwelu. Somehow, Panellinios of Greece had obtained the services of 400H World Record Holder Kevin Young. The Sunday was going to be interesting.

A good start

The match day dawned bright. There was not a breath of wind as temperatures soared and tree pollen fell intrusively like thick snowflakes. Several athletes succumbed to hay fever and were in such a bad way they needed medical treatment. Your team managers refused to let anyone be injected by the match doctors so it was cold wet towels and a prayer for those suffering.

Realising a good start was essential we left early for the hammer competition. Not only was this not to be in the main Stadium but we were soon to discover there was no cage either. The competitors agreed to throw but it was the foot fault officials who won the plaudits. Luckily for everyone the throwers were careful but there could have been some serious injuries. Mick Jones threw exceptionally well in the circumstances with 3 throws over 70m and his longest, at 72.55m gaining second spot and 7 points – a decent start.

Whereas the hammer had started at 10.00am the main programme started in the main Stadium at 16.40 to avoid the highest temperatures and to satisfy TV.

We had taken advantage of the time gap to practice sprint relay takeovers and get in some starting practice. Darren Campbell was looking lethargic out of the blocks and we hoped race conditions would improve his reactions.

But first to the pole vault. It was about now that we realised the large local crowd were not interested in good competition and sportsmanship. Partizan was an appropriate name for both the local club and supporters. First into the gladiatorial ring was Mike Edwards, not someone to be easily intimidated. As the competition progressed the crowd hurled abuse at all but their man. Mike loved it. The louder they shouted the perkier he got. Second place and another 7 points. Good man, but Partizan were first as they were in the next event the discus. Kevin Brown seemed to hurry his throws and finished fourth – one place lower than he could have expected. Nonetheless 5 more points.

We lead after six events

The long jump started as the pole vault reached its final stages. The runways were separated by a few metres and Julian Flynn was clearly affected by the crowd. Adrenaline rush or whatever, his only valid jump was in the 5th round by which time the team managers were reduced to nervous wrecks. Fifth place and four points. Then to the first track event the 400 flat. Brankovic was Partizan's Captain, the 1992 European Champion and the crowd's favourite. A long hold on the track in fierce heat saw the opposition wilt before the start

and it was the Yugoslav who rushed into a commanding lead. Sean Baldock looked jaded and the crowds support for their man reached deafening proportions as he entered the home straight with a 5m lead. But what was this? Relentlessly inch by inch Sean was pulling back the big Yugoslav only to pass him with a desperate lunge 5m before the tape. The crowd groaned and we had recorded our first and only victory of the day.

In the 100m, Darren Campell lived up to our worst fears and had a disastrous start. Seventh after 30m he then got into that liquid smooth, ground eating stride and was gaining fast on all but the leader as the line was reached. 10.03 was the winning time by Obikwelu with Darren in third. Twenty minutes later Andy Tulloch lined up against Schwarthoff in the high hurdles and was rewarded with a fine second spot in a legal 13.63.

After six events we were leading with 41 points followed by Partizan on 39 and Lac Quelle Munich, the German Champions on 33. From then on it was mostly downhill. Stuart Faben managed just 2 throws in the javelin and should have finished at least 2 places higher than 5th. Matt Kloiber faded badly after leading the 800 for 600m and could only manage 7th. Then Philips Idowu withdrew from the triple jump after 3 attempts due to a slight injury. His best of 14.81 was probably his poorest competition of the year and only good enough for 7th spot. The abyss was beckoning. We realised it wasn't our day in the sprint relay when the second leg runner outside Jason Fergus pulled a hamstring and pushed our man across the track. With a side-step worthy of a rugby veteran, Jason continued and we recovered to 4th with a commendable 40.53. But again with a bit of luck it could have been so much better.

Challenger close to 2.31m

In the high jump things were looking better. Ben Challenger, like Mike Edwards appeared to be gaining inspiration from the raucous crowd. His 2.28 pb clearance was effortless and one of the 2.31 attempts was as close as you could get to a clearance without actually succeeding. Nevertheless, 2nd spot, 7 points and what a debut for Belgrave. James Ellis Smith went with the leaders in a very hot 5000. Castro of Sporting Club of Lisbon got outkicked as James succumbed to the heat and travelling and faded serenely to 6th spot. Matt Douglas struggled to find form in the 400H as Kevin Young cruised to 48.88 at the age of 32. Still plenty of time Matt!

Next came Jason Fergus in the 200 still shaken after the sprint relay traumas. Actually, shaken and stirred by the looks of things, as he gained the bronze medal in a very creditable 21.01 legal. Obikwelu again won, this time in 20.50.

Lee Newman performed well up to expectations in the shot that was won with a massive 20.71 by Peric of Partizan. This match was hot!

Lee Hurst then stepped up for the steeplechase and, putting all thoughts of partying behind him, showed true grit to again get a bronze medal. His performance was clearly the best of the middle distance men who generally had found the long journey just too much – witness Jason Lobo fading to 7th in the 1500 and like Matt Kloiber mystified that there was no strength when he needed it. And so to the last event the long relay. Geoff Dearman and Michael Parper joined the drained Matt Douglas and Sean Baldock to finish with another bronze, by which time we had faded to 4th. With only two teams promoted we realised that this had been one of the toughest matches we had been in.

Overall, 10 Group 'B' records had been set in this cauldron of a stadium. Considering that this event has been going for 25 years, this was a truly competitive and high standard match. There is no reason to believe it will be any easier in 1999 but you can bet that should we be there, we will be stronger and better prepared.

Bill Laws

Notice · page 24

End of the road for Larios

It was reported in Athletics International recently that as from November 1998 the Spanish drinks manufacturer Larios would not be continuing its sponsorship of the top Spanish athletics club that bears its name.

It seems that Larios have poured some $1 million per year into the club since 1985 resulting in a massive international recruitment campaign that brought them Merlene Ottey, Javier Sotomayor, Brahim Boutayeb, Samuel Matete, James Beckford and a large proportion of the Cuban national team. Not surprisingly this tide of athletic talent took them to first place in six out of seven European Champion Clubs Cup competitions between 1990 and 1996. They were then second in 1997 and only fourth in 1998.

The club, based in the Madrid suburb of Moratalaz were known to be searching for another sponsor with the possibility of disbanding altogether if another cash provider was not forthcoming.

Merlene OtteyJavier SotomayorBrahim BoutayebSamuel MateteJames Beckford

Feature · page 24

British Embassy Comes Up Trumps

We knew the British Embassy in Belgrade was good. Even before our ill-fated trip they had been in touch to advise us that the direct flight from Gatwick would not be operated. A fact that BA only acknowledged a couple of weeks before our departure.

Anyway, Bob Gordon, the Consul, invited the team and some of our Jugoslav hosts to the British Embassy for an excellent barbeque on the eve of our match. While Michael Parper led the 'horse races' for the children, Marlon Dickson worked his way through three meals. Up 'til then he had proclaimed a distinct dislike for local food and was on hunger strike.

The numerous and huge bowls of strawberries and cream crowned a great evening. Mick Jones had been explaining to anyone who would listen that his training at Ockley Court Farm took place on a smooth concrete pad distanced some 75 metres from the nearest strawberry bed. So Mike's claim to fame was to reach the strawberries. No sooner the word! Team members were seen to smuggle out bowls of strawberries, not for consumption, but to give Mick some added incentive during the next day's competition. And you will see from the results that it worked.

Bob GordonMichael ParperMarlon DicksonMick JonesMike

Notice · page 24

See you in Athens?

The 1999 European Champion Clubs Cup should make for an interesting weekend at the end of May. It seems that all divisions—men and women— will be held at the same stadium in Athens. No doubt there will be a hard core of Belgrave supporters making the trip to support the Bels. If you are interested, make sure you let Bill Laws know.

Report · page 25

Winning ways at Watford

Bill Laws

British League Division One - Track & Field

Winning ways at Watford

MATCH 1, WATFORD, 16MAY 1998

This was a TVH home fixture. The 'Valley' have been having problems with their local Council and the once pristine West London Stadium has now become unusable for League fixtures. It is tragic that after the euphoria of re-naming it in honour of Linford Christie, the Council should keep its hands in its pockets and let it slide into gentle decline.

So here we were at a new venue which proved to be very congenial, blessed with fine weather and eight teams eager for the start of a new season.

Dave Smith, competing for the first time since breaking a bone in his foot was highly delighted to win the 'A' hammer. John Urquhart returned after a winter's retirement to win the 'B' event and so ensure that as usual the hammer squad got us off to a great start. The long jump proved a disaster in more ways than one with only three competitors clearing 7 metres and Commonwealth Games representative Chris Davison of Newham winning with a meagre 7.27m. The triple jump distances were also down and the flat jumps run-up was branded one of the slowest in the South by all competitors. Something to note if you're looking for a good competition!

Good points in the first track race, the 400H, kept us in contention as the team scores were updated. Matt Douglas nudged towards his PB in his first outing of the year while Ian Wells pointed out that his 'B' selection was the first by right for three years. Ever a willing reserve, this promotion encouraged a pleasing 4th place.

Following his tester in the previous week's Southern League, Darren Campbell won the 'A' 100 in style clocking 10.48 seconds into a 1.2m/s headwind. Jason Fergus was pipped by Alan Condon in the 'B' – but excellent points.

Chris Rawlinson moved down to the sprint hurdles and 200m but it was clear that a long layoff with injury had affected his sharpness. Little were we to know that Chris' troubles would be increasing as the season progressed. Wins for Brendan Reilly in the high jump, Geoff Dearman in the flat 400, and by both vaulters and both shot putters made up for a disaster in the javelin and a dropped baton in the sprint relay. A fine win in the long relay eased the team to victory. A final leg by Sean Baldock timed at 45.7 was the highlight. Clearly the Baldock brothers are going to be a big asset in the years ahead.

Thanks to the many youngsters who filled the gaps. With the likes of Charles Igbon, Scott Exley, Steve Scott, Samson Oni and Philips Idowu providing major back-up, we were going to be difficult to beat.

Report · page 25

Record 4 x 400 metres but Shaftesbury Barnet make their point

Bill Laws

MATCH 2, BIRMINGHAM, 6 JUN 1998

So much for my predictions.

We lost this one by a single point – surprisingly to Shaftesbury Barnet who had only managed 5th place in Match 1. Once again the League was wide open and we would need to make a concerted effort in the next two matches.

It wasn't difficult to find excuses. The previous week's ECCCF match in Belgrade had taken its toll – particularly on the middle distance men. Our paucity of depth in middle distance was cruelly exposed and we could not complete 'B' events in the 800,1500, 3000 steeplechase and 5000m. Ian Wells gamely started the 3000 steeplechase but was forced out with injury. Steve Baldock ran the 'A' 800 and 'A' 5000!

But now for the good news. The hammer was first class with Mick Jones throwing a PB to beat Paul Head with both men over 73 metres. Matt Douglas clocked the same time as in Match 1 to win the 'A' 400H. Darren Campbell and Jason Fergus ensured good points in the 100 but a poor start cost Darren victory. Dwain Chambers made a rare appearance in the 200 and the dream sprint relay squad of Chambers, Fergus, Walcott and Campbell set an impressive early season time of 40.51 as they deadheated with the Newham boys. Even the camera couldn't separate the two teams.

The even better news was that several newcomers made a noticeable difference. Julian Flynn, in both the long jump and triple jump, is going to be a major points contributor, Richard Sear had his first taste of Division 1 sprint hurdles and junior javelin thrower, Tim Kitney pipped stalwart Stuart Faben to provide a welcome points boost. Ben Challenger made his League high jump début with a steady 2.20m. With Brendan Reilly still sidelined, suffering from whiplash injuries, Samson Oni got carried away in the exalted company and opened at 1.95m – which he failed to clear.

Ian Tullett turned the tables on Mike Edwards and once again we had maximum points in the pole vault.

But the best news was a new club record in the long relay. Michael Parper, Geoff Dearman, Matt Douglas and Sean Baldock knew that only a win would give us a chance of victory. Their 3.09.26 provided a 2 second margin over second spot but it was Shaftesbury Barnet in second place which ensured they won the match by just 1 point. Ian Wells blamed his failure in the steeplechase for our demise but it was the absence of most of our recognised middle distance men that had placed an unequal burden on the rest of the team.

Report · page 26

Wrapped up for '98?

Track & Field - British League Division One

Wrapped up for '98?

MATCH 3, EDINBURGH, 4 JUL 1998

Although Edinburgh is a wonderful city this is a trip we prefer to avoid. Partly because it effectively means going up on a Friday and returning Sunday but mainly due to the enormous cost of travel. Shaftesbury and Newham flew up and estimated the cost of this match alone at £6,500 each. We travelled by train and stayed at Edinburgh University Halls of Residence which kept our costs to a modest £3,600! Bearing in mind that seven teams travelled, the total cost to all concerned was over £20,000. With no League sponsor and dwindling reserves, more attention will need to be paid by the British Athletics League to suitable venues for the 1999 season.

On the bright side we had a great trip, good team spirit and a night in Edinburgh after the match where the taverns were full of revellers watching the World Cup Football on large screens. Tough old life isn't it?

The weather had been bad north of the border this summer and by all accounts we had the best of it. Force 5 winds and intermittent squalls. Some of the events were of a remarkably high standard considering the conditions. Jason Lobo doubled up on his first League appearance for us and finished a fine second in the 'A' 800m with a surprised Kevin McKay of Sale back in 5th spot. Kevin made amends by winning the 'A' 1500m but newcomer Mark Miles caused a stir by hanging on until the last 200 where lack of training told and he drifted back to 4th spot. Immediately afterwards Jason again collected silver, this time in the 'B' 1500m to total 24 points in his first League outing. Matt Yates made his only appearance for us this year to win the 'B" 800m.

In the throws we welcomed another newcomer Nick Sweeney. Nick couldn't compete for us last year due to an operation but proved his value by winning the 'A' discus and finishing second in the 'A' shot for big points score (30). Ironically, Matt Simson was still sidelined due to the elbow operation which he had after Match 1.

A blast from the past was Fred Salle, surprising everyone to win the 'A' long jump. This allowed Julian Flynn to win the 'B' long jump, so more good points. With Fred around people were inquiring whether this was Commonwealth year?

Chris Rawlinson won the 'A' 400H in a gamble to improve fitness before the AAA's Championships. He was still treading a very thin line between fitness and injury.

Welcome back too to Haroun Korjie and Marlon Dickson in the sprints. Unfortunately, Haroun found the cold conditions unsettling and tore a hamstring in the sprint relay. Mind you it didn't stop him clubbing in the evening. And who else made a welcome return? None other than Neil Owen, glad once again to be sprint hurdling. After two frustrating injury ruined years, the 'long un' was back and pleased to be part of the team once more. With Nick Cooper also back from injury the sprint hurdles look brighter.

Brendan Reilly too was back after his car accident and although he won the 'A' high jump, was not happy after failing at 2.20m. So much so that the large arc transcribed by his take-off shoe would have been a stadium throws record, except for the assisting wind. We avoided him for 5 minutes thereafter!

Much better points in the steeplechase moved us into the lead in the late afternoon drizzle. Lee Hurst turned in his usual first class performance and newcomer Kevin Nash looked the part as he won the 'B' event.

The match was well and truly won by the time the relays came round, which was just as well, as a combination of injuries and tiredness ensured that no records were in danger.

Behind us the other teams were playing musical chairs which ensured that our lead extended to 5 League points which virtually wrapped up the competition. But I've been wrong before and Match 4 would still be a test of will.

Report · page 27

'JR' is back- 20.56! Reilly gets 'Euro' qualifier

British League Division One - Track & Field

'JR' is back- 20.56! Reilly gets 'Euro' qualifier

MATCH 4, COPTHALL, 8 AUG 1998

As we approached this match the AAA's Championships were well and truly done and dusted. There remained a few places yet to be filled for the European championships and the Commonwealth Games. Jason Lobo was chasing an 800m qualifying time in Brussels while Matt Yates was chasing a 1500m time in Spain. So once again we had a depleted middle distance squad.

Also chasing a high jump qualifying mark was Brendan Reilly and Match 4 represented his last chance with final selections taking place that evening. True to form, Brendan produced the goods clearing 2.28 and narrowly failing at 2.35. A very happy bunny!

But this was after a dream start with Mick Jones and Dave Smith going head to head to win maximum points in the hammer. Dave's 70.84m was best to date indicating a gradual return to fitness but he was still to take second to Mick, although he did get the valuable scalp of Newham's Paul Head. Good points too in the long jump but conditions and distances were poor.

Kevin Brown continued to improve in the discus pushing the 59 metre mark but still down on qualifying requirements for England for the Commonwealth Games. Lee Newman, already picked for Wales couldn't get motivated but won the 'B' division.

Excellent points came from both sprints, with John Regis making a welcome League return in the 200m after a gap of Lord knows how many years. Anyway, we were delighted to see him back in the team and he seemed to be pleased he came, after running a superb 20.56 which was to ensure his Commonwealth selection.

Soon after, the sprint relay squad were being wound up by the Newham quartet who had dead heated with them in Match 2. This seemed to fire our boys . Junior Chris Lambert ran a superb first leg and John Regis responded with a huge rush down the back straight. Clean takeovers by Drew Walcott and Jason Fergus saw a season's best time of 40.43 with the Newham boys 5m down.

And finally to the long relay which was to be a formality as our Match victory and League title had been assured much earlier in the afternoon. Newham finished runners up in the League and look to be the main opposition next year. Our total points score of 1460 was 55 more than last year and was our highest ever.

And this from our youngest team too! The signs are good for next year. I'm looking forward to it already. Hope you are too because if there's one thing the team enjoys, it's your support. With more London fixtures next year, there's no excuse for not coming along and meeting the team.

Report · page 28

Three Gold Cup wins in a row

Track & Field - Gold Cup

Three Gold Cup wins in a row

With 2 years as Cup Winners behind us we were conscious that a third consecutive win would represent a feat only previously achieved by Haringey AC at the height of their powers.

SEMI-FINAL, WAVERTREE, LIVERPOOL, 19 JUL 1998

Two years ago when we had a cup semi at Liverpool we were sent the wrong timetable and arrived after the Match had started. We eventually scraped home in second place.

We didn't intend to cut it so fine this time and despite other competing events we were determined not to take chances. Thankfully a number of team members put Club before Area and we sat back to witness some first class performances in difficult conditions. I say, we, because Sir Eddie had jetted into the UK just to support the team and in John Jeffery's absence he was co-opted as Joint Team Manager. Taking the final result into account Sir Eddie thought both he and we had done rather well.

That is a bit of an understatement because despite the presence of two other BAL Division 1 teams (Sale and Border), we won 16 of the 20 events with an all time record score of 167 points. Bearing in mind that to win all 20 events would give 180 points you can see that this was pretty impressive stuff.

The results speak for themselves but the highlights were: Matt Simson's first outing since his elbow operation which limited him to the single winning effort in the shot. Mark Miles moved down to the 800m and then won the 5000m in a personal best. Well it had to be a PB because it was the first one he had run. Afterwards he said he thought it was a jog although the sprint at the end was interesting. Jim Estall was the oldest on display but moved nicely to win the 10,000m. He puts the new fluency down to an extensive stretching programme, although there is no verification of room mate James Ellis Smith's assertion, that this routine started at 5.00 in the morning.

We welcomed our newest and youngest member into the team – Ben Flint – who won the pole vault in difficult conditions.

Anyway, an excellent result and another one for the record books.

HT: 1, M.Jones 72.06. 4x100m: 1, Belgrave (N.Owen, A.Walcott, J.Flynn, J.Fergus) 42.9. 4x400m: 3, Belgrave ( M.Douglas, J.Ellis Smith, I.Wells, G.Dearman) 3:26.0.

Teams: 1, Belgrave 167; 2, Sale Manchester 124; 3, Border 90; 4, Liverpool 87; =5, City of Stoke & Sheffield 74; 7, Trafford 72; 8, Morpeth 17.

FINAL, BEDFORD, 29 AUG 1998

Consternation! A few weeks before the Cup Final, Athletics 98 announced a GB/USA match in Glasgow for 1st September. Immediately we knew that some CUP FINAL team members would be called up. Luckily we persuaded the powers that be to delay flying most of their team from Heathrow until the last flight which was about 8.00pm. The only person we lost was Matt Yates due to some ruling that he couldn't compete on consecutive days. Mind you, Kevin Mackay did, so we never got to the bottom of that one.

Anyway, we moved Jason Lobo to the 1500m, put Matt Kloiber in the 800m and persuaded Mark Miles to run his second 5000m and we were sorted. All we waited for now, was to see how Newham had been affected by the USA match. And as the afternoon unfolded it became clear that Newham could scrape a win.

The start was nip and tuck. Mick Jones won the hammer in another PB. Paul Head of Newham second. Julian Flynn then had an unexpected win in the long jump over Newham's Chris Davison who was destined for Kuala Lumpur. Then the 10,000m where Newham's Ian Grime outkicked Kassa. Our 3 best shots and we were just a couple of points ahead.

Newham then won the 400H and the 800m and we were in second spot with the rest nowhere. Ben Challenger competing for the first time since injuring himself in the Europa Cup, produced a stunning 2.26 to win the high jump but the Newham man was third. The discus was a 1,2 with Nick Sweeney well ahead of Newham's Paul Head.

The 100m turned out to be historic due to the unexpected return of Linford Christie. His win had no effect on the team positions but Drew Walcott was a place behind the Newham man. Newham then pipped our men in the 110H, 400m and triple jump and we trailed by 6 points. Lee Hurst reduced the deficit in the 3000 s/c and Jason Lobo pulled back more points to finish a fine second in the 1500m to Kevin McKay.

Birchfield then came to our aid in the shot with an unknown American outthrowing Newham Mark Proctor with Matt Simson third. Then it was down to John Regis who, although pipped by Sale's Allyn Condon, managed to squeeze 6 points out of the Newham man. The javelin saw Newham's Mark Robinson level the scores but then Mike Edwards' win in the pole vault and Mark Miles' win in the 5000m saw us start to ease away. The 5000m was thrilling and a slow tactical race saw a great burn up at the end. One which Mark Miles won, beating the favourite Darius Burrows with a devastating 1.58.0 last 800m. A fine win in the sprint relay widened our lead. We used the same team as Match 4 League with Chris Lambert as lead-off and then the long relay boys clinched a memorable victory. Good to see Dave Nolan run the first leg in his only outing of the season ruined by injury.

Another brilliant win which from the result looks easy but which went to the wire.

Track & Field - continues on page 30

Feature · page 29

From Your Album

From Charlie Manning's album ...

Above: Chas (18) tears into the lead on the first lap of a Club Championship 880 yards Race at Battersea Park. We are not absolutely certain of the year but it was probably 1955, one of the years when Charlie Walker, (4) at the back of the pack became Club Champion over the distance. Behind Charlie Manning can be seen a youthful Jim Heathfield. Can anyone put names to the other faces in the picture?

Above: It's first day of May 1954 and the smiling band above are 5 to 6 miles into the Surrey 20 miles Road Race Championship held at Mitcham. Chas. (12) was 23rd gaining a Surrey County Standard with a time of 2:14:10. Len Adams (16) and John Baker (15) can also be identified.

Above: Fortythree years later and the old codger's still on the move! 24th August 1997 and Chas. (174) lines up for the Veterans A.C. 1500 metres Championship at Kingsmeadow. His time was ... well we won't go into that ... but is this a good case for euthanasia? Maybe we should pay him not to wear our club kit! Seriously though, 1,500 metres is now way over his best distance because in 1998 Chas became the Surrey County M70 Champion over 100 metres!

Third from the left, standing back, is Dave McMullen and wearing 62 is 5,000 metre ace from the '50s and current UK Athletics Council Member - Chris Chataway.

Do you have any pictures from your album that you'd like to lend us?

Charlie ManningCharlie WalkerJim HeathfieldLen AdamsJohn BakerDave McMullenChris ChatawayEd LaceyJeremy Hemming

Notice · page 29

Belgrave Web Site - coming soon

Charlie Manning has very kindly offered to sponsor our proposed Belgrave Harriers Web Site which should be up and running this summer. Thanks to Chas., and while we are at it, many thanks to all those generous souls whose donations keep The Belgravian in business. Readers are often surprised to find that the costs of our gazette are met solely out of the normal running costs of the Club together with donations from members. Actually, we do get a few donations from non-members who like to read our mag. Recently a copy was sent to a prospective member - who subsequently did not join - but his coach was so taken with it and enjoyed his read so much that he sent in a donation of £10. Thank you Barry Tilbury (who passes on his best wishes to any that remember him). Receiving his letter of appreciation and donation was one of those moments when one realises why 'one does it'.

Charlie ManningBarry Tilbury

Notice · page 29

and thanks to Charlie Dickinson

Unfortunately Charlie Dickinson has had to bow out as Joint Editor of The Belgravian due to work commitments. Charlie provided the link between the Joe Coleman years and the magazine in its current format, edited by Alan Mead and himself. All members will want to thank Chas. for providing that valuable link and your remaining Editor certainly wishes to record his appreciation.

Charlie DickinsonJoe ColemanAlan Mead

Report · page 30

Its Tough In Two !

Jacqui Smiter

Track & Field - Women's Southern League Division Two

Its Tough In Two !

— Reports by Jacqui Smiter —

MATCH 1, BATTERSEA PK., 25 APR 1998

The weather was good for the opening match of the season. Expectations were that we would "make an impression" but lesson number one in Division Two was learned pretty quickly: it's a lot tougher than Division Three! From the very first event when Thurrock's Gowry Retchakan blitzed away from the 400 hurdles field it was a long hard battle to stay anywhere near being in touch. We had a good senior team with some new athletes making their débuts — and even had a reasonable turn-out of under 15s but the opposition was just too strong and we had to settle for fourth team place.

MATCH 2, LINFORD CHRISTIE STADIUM, 30 MAY 1998

Another good team of seniors travelled to West London for match two but this time we had only three under 15s and as a result the team could only get up to 4th position again and at the end of the day hovered four places from the bottom of the division.

Those under 15s might have been few in number but they did perform well. Both Rebecca Lester and Michelle Nestor set personal bests in the 200 metres while Louise Collier reduced her best 800 metres by a whole 15 seconds.

Hounslow, international Lyn Sprules threw a massive 58.20 metres in the hammer. For the Belles, new member Vicky Hassell upped her best ever performances in the javelin (by 4 metres), discus (another 4 metres) and shot (1 metre).

MATCH 3, ENFIELD, 27 JUN 1998

Constant rain throughout the afternoon made our plight even more miserable and with key people missing from the team we sank to 5th place. It rained so hard at one point that events had to be held up until the deluge eased off. One of those afternoons when it was good to get home!

MATCH 4, WALTON, 18 JUL 1998

Nearer to home at Walton we managed to notch up a lot of individual 2nd places in our battle to pull back some points. In our younger age group we even gained some wins but try as hard as we could, we still couldn't break out of 4th team place. The fight to avoid bouncing straight back to Division Three was beginning to look impossible to win.

One of the highlights of the afternoon was a double win from Rebecca Lester and Louise Collier in the under 15 800 metres, and Rebecca was involved in another double win when together with Michelle Nestor, they cleaned up in the high jump.

MATCH 5, PORTSMOUTH, 22 AUG 98

Up against division leaders Horsham Blue Star, the team did really well, starting off the afternoon by holding the Sussex club back and actually leading the match for a while. You can't say the Belles don't try! The 400 metres was particularly exciting and had a strong Belgrave interest as Sarah Roberts fought off Horsham's (and second-claim Belgrave's) Vicky Pincott. In the 'B' race it was the same again as Marcella Simon again held Horsham at bay. Sabrina Scott gained good points in the sprints and then took just two jumps to mop up the 'B' long jump while Angela Abrams gained maximum points in the 'A' competition. A double win came our way in the pole vault thanks to Julia Cockram and Maureen Jones — but in spite of all this, in a desperately close match, were again 4th.

MATCH 6, ST. IVES, HUNTINGDON, 5 SEP 1998

One day we'll have a fine day at this Huntingdonshire track. With hardly any shelter and the rain sweeping across the stadium all afternoon it was another soaking for the Belles as they put in their best efforts in the search for points. The pole vault and under 15 high jump were both cancelled due to the dangerous conditions — a shame because these are two of our strongest events — and even Sarah Roberts had a tough day, unable to hold on to the lead in the 400 metres. We had a few wins in the 'B' string events and a double win in the senior high jump but with yet another 4th team place we had to face up to the fact that it was going to be back to Division Three for us next season.

Report · page 32

Southern League Bels unbeaten in 1998

Track & Field - Men's Southern League Division Two

Southern League Bels unbeaten in 1998

LADYWELL, 2 MAY 1998

A tremendous string of results came from our Men's Southern League Team in 1998. From the instant that the gun was fired in the very first race at Ladywell it was Belgrave all the way, highlights being a Division Record from Darren Campbell in the 200 metres and a nice series of performances from a man getting into Decathlon mode — Du'aine Ladejo. Simon Shirley, decathlete and Commonwealth Games medallist, was also on hand to push the points score along but not all records were set by those with an eye on international events later in the season — John Druce celebrated his entry into the veteran ranks with an M40 Triple Jump club record.

After the first match Belgrave headed the Division thanks to a massive haul of 146 points.

100m: 1, J.Fergus 11.1; 2, D.Akinbogun 11.9. 200m: 1, D.Campbell 21.5; 1, M.Parper 22.9. 400m: 1, J.Hilston 50.5; 1, R.David 51.1. 800m: 1, C.Gilby 1:57.5; 1, S.Baldock 2:00.2. 1500m: 1, C.Gilby 4:07.8; 1, R.Alsop 4:07.9. 5000m: 4, R.Metalle 17:30.4; 4, D.Anderson 17:52.7. 110mH: 1, D.Ladejo 15.7; 1, S.Exley 16.5. 400mH: 1, J.Squirrell 57.5; 2, D.Crook 67.1. 3000mSC: 4, P.MacDonald 11:25.9. Teams: 1, Belgrave 146; 2, Kent 118; 3, Epsom & Ewell 109; 4, Cambridge & Coleridge 97; 5, London Irish 42.

BATTERSEA PK, 16 MAY 1998

A home meeting gave the Bels a chance to pile on even more pressure in the push for promotion, our final tally being a huge 153 points. That must surely be the most we have ever scored in a Southern League match. It was nice to pick up maximum scores in the 1500 metres — we don't do that too often. Until the last time down the back straight it looked young Kevin Quinn's race but then Ray Foley threw in a blistering finish to gain a clear win. There was more fine sprinting from Ladejo and Marlon Dickson but once again two veterans got on the scoring sheet in a big way as Jim Estall and Steve Fowler took both first places in the 5k. Actually, we say two veterans but what about that Mike Small? At every single match he tackles two, three or even four throws — and usually picks up top points. He's 45 come 1999 and is still shooting for lifetime pbs.

MILTON KEYNES, 6 JUN 1998

At Milton Keynes much of the pre-match discussion was focused on "who was going to do our 400 metres 'A' string — Richard David or James Hilston. James has always had the edge in the past but this year has been suffering from a stomach disorder which has restricted his training and consequently his race performances. Richard, on the other hand, is looking stronger and faster than ever and was not far short of beating Michael Parper in the Surrey Champs. Somehow or other they came to an agreement — and then both of them got beaten by a very powerful Milton Keynes pair! Ray Foley ran for a point in the 800m prior to getting outgunned over the last half lap in the 'A' 1500m. The steeplechase was next up and as this match was looking a lot tighter than the previous two, he decided he'd have another run round for a point. As the race progressed he gained in confidence and technique and ended up leaving the meeting pondering an athletics career move to the 'chase. James Tuson turned on a double win in high jump and triple — where John Druce again upped his veteran record.

BATTERSEA PK., 4 JUL 1998

Back at Battersea for match four and up against our main rivals Newham & Essex Beagles, another huge win more or less sealed our promotion prospects. Throwers Lee Newman and Leith Marar made welcome SL appearances and together with Mike Small and, surprisingly, Michael Peacock the heavy events were big earners. Lee's 59.51 discus throw was a division record. Andrew Walcott spiced up the sprints and again the 5,000 metres gave us a maximum as Gary Staines and Jim Estall ran away from the field. It was Junior Galley's turn to impress in the steeplechase as both he and Marcello Bizio battled to gain 'A' string honours.

PORTSMOUTH, 15 AUG 1998

... And the answer was, "Yes" — a maximum possible score of 30 league points had been achieved. However, we must not lose sight of the fact that this, our furthest match from London, saw us turn up with no 5,000 metre runners, just one 1,500 metre man, one 'chaser and a veteran triple jumper our only "competitor" in the 800 metres. Even our two 400 metre hurdlers were pressganged. There is no way that we will survive in Division One next season unless we get some middle distance men prepared to travel. It was good to see Nick Dawson back in action over 200 metres and James Flavin had a great afternoon: his first ever 400 metres hurdles resulted in a fine win in 58.9 and for good measure he ran 22.6 over half a lap for more top points. James Hilston's health problems, whilst restricting his 400 metres outings, did at least give him the chance to tackle a few more rarely attempted events — and he came up with an impressive triple jump win in 13.55. Let's pay tribute to a couple of other men who always get near the front and who have contributed so much to the season's success — sprinters Dotun Akinbogun and Joe Panton. Both did the business in their individual events and then contributed greatly to further maximums in both relays.

Report · page 33

Touch and go - but the girls just miss out

JUBILEE CUP, FIRST ROUND, DEANGATE, 14 JUN 1998

With just one per event there were high hopes that we might just sneak through into the second round of this knockout competition — even though only the winners progress. It was touch and go throughout the afternoon with good points collected by Sabrina Scott in the sprints and Sarah Roberts in the 400 metres and triple jump. Even joint team manager Jacqui Smiter got in on the winning act by picking up top score in the pole vault but in the end it was the local club that prevailed. If we'd had one strong middle distance runner we probably would have done it. All credit to young Debbie Hearn for turning out in the 1500 metres even though she was suffering from a bruised head, the result of a crash in Dad's van!
